Non-Stationary Difference Equation and Affine Laumon Space II: Quantum Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov Equation
Hidetoshi AWATA a, Koji HASEGAWA b, Hiroaki KANNO ac, Ryo OHKAWA de, Shamil SHAKIROV fg, Jun’ichi SHIRAISHI h and Yasuhiko YAMADA i

Abstract
We show that Shakirov’s non-stationary difference equation, when it is truncated, implies the quantum Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov ( q q -KZ) equation for U 𝗏 ​ ( A 1 ( 1 ) ) U_{\mathsf{v}}\bigl(A_{1}^{(1)}\bigr) with generic spins. Namely, we can tune mass parameters so that the Hamiltonian acts on the space of finite Laurent polynomials. Then the representation matrix of the Hamiltonian agrees with the R R -matrix, or the quantum 6 ​ j 6j symbols. On the other hand, we prove that the K K theoretic Nekrasov partition function from the affine Laumon space is identified with the well-studied Jackson integral solution to the q q -KZ equation. Combining these results, we establish that the affine Laumon partition function gives a solution to Shakirov’s equation, which was a conjecture in our previous paper. We also work out the base-fiber duality and four-dimensional limit in relation with the q q -KZ equation.



keywords
affine Laumon space; quantum affine algebra; non-stationary difference equation; quantum Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov equation

1
Introduction

The quantum (or
q
-deformed) Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov (
q
-KZ) equation typically arises as a linear difference equation
satisfied by matrix elements of the products of intertwining operators of highest weight representations
of quantum affine algebra
[
23
,
27
]
.
It roughly takes the following form:

\displaystyle\Psi(u_{1},\dots,pu_{j},\dots,u_{n})

\displaystyle{}=R_{V_{j}V_{j-1}}\biggl(\frac{pu_{j}}{u_{j-1}}\biggr)\cdots R_{V_{j}V_{1}}\biggl(\frac{pu_{j}}{u_{1}}\biggr)D_{j}

\displaystyle\quad{}\times R_{V_{n}V_{j}}^{-1}\biggl(\frac{u_{n}}{u_{j}}\biggr)\cdots R_{V_{j+1}V_{j}}^{-1}\biggl(\frac{u_{j+1}}{u_{j}}\biggr)\Psi(u_{1},\dots,u_{n}),

(1.1)

where
\Psi(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
is an appropriate matrix element of the intertwiners with the spectral parameters
u_{i}

and
R_{V_{k}V_{\ell}}
denotes the
R
matrix on the tensor product
V_{k}\otimes V_{\ell}
of highest weight representations.

D_{j}
is a diagonal operator acting on
V_{j}
. In the case of
U_{\mathsf{v}}\bigl(A^{(1)}\bigr)
with level
k
, the shift parameter is fixed to be
p={\sf v}^{2(k+2)}=q^{k+2}
.
1
1

1

The convention used in our papers
[
10
,
51
]
is different from
the standard convention for the deformation parameter of the quantum affine algebra.
This is the reason why we denote
U_{\mathsf{v}}\bigl(A_{1}^{(1)}\bigr)
with
{\sf v}^{2}=q
.
According to the AGT dictionary for the current algebra
[
6
]
,

k+2=-\epsilon_{2}/\epsilon_{1}
, where

(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})
are the
\Omega
-background parameters on the gauge theory side.
Hence, we have
p=e^{(k+2)\epsilon_{1}}=e^{-\epsilon_{2}}=t
.

The case
n=2
is of our interest in this paper.
By the scaling of the spectral parameters, which fixes the origin and the infinity,
we can consider the two point function
\Phi(u):=\Psi(1,u)
, for which the
q
-KZ equation is

\Phi(pu)=R_{V_{2}V_{1}}(pu)D_{2}\Phi(u).

(1.2)

For the
q
-difference equations including (
1.2
),
various important results using the Jackson integrals have been developed based on the fundamental theory by K. Aomoto.
In particular, the Jackson integral of symmetric Selberg type relevant for the equation (
1.2
)
was thoroughly studied by Aomoto, Kato, Matsuo, Mimachi and Ito
around early 1990’s (see, e.g.,
[
8
,
29
]
and references therein).
Around the same time, the relation to Bethe vectors was found by Reshetikhin
[
48
]
and the method
was further developed by Tarasov, Varchenko, etc.
These works play a crucial role in our study.

In
[
10
]
, we show that the non-stationary difference equation proposed in
[
51
]

is transformed into a quantization of the discrete Painlevé VI equation.
The original form of the non-stationary difference equation is

\displaystyle\mathsf{U}(t\Lambda,x)=\mathcal{A}_{1}(\Lambda,x)\cdot\mathcal{B}\cdot\mathcal{A}_{2}(\Lambda,x)\cdot\mathcal{B}\cdot\mathcal{A}_{3}(\Lambda,x)\mathsf{U}\biggl(\Lambda,{x\over tqQ}\biggr),

(1.3)

where
\mathcal{B}
is the
q
-Borel transformation on a formal Laurent series in
x
:

\mathcal{B}\biggl(\sum_{n}c_{n}x^{n}\biggr)=\sum_{n}q^{n(n+1)/2}c_{n}x^{n},

(1.4)

and
\mathcal{A}_{i}(\Lambda,x)
are products of infinite products
(x;q)_{\infty}

and
(x;q,t)_{\infty}
defined by (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), respectively.
(See
[
51
]
and
[
10
]
for explicit forms of
\mathcal{A}_{i}(\Lambda,x)
).
In Appendix
A
, we explain a relation of the operator
\mathcal{B}
and the refined Chern–Simons theory
[
2
]
,
which gave the original motivation for introducing
\mathcal{B}
in
[
51
]
.
The non-stationary difference equation (
1.3
) was found by looking at the instanton partition function of
five-dimensional
{\rm SU}(2)
gauge theory with a surface defect.
The parameters
(q,t)
are equivariant parameters of the torus action
(z_{1},z_{2})\longrightarrow\bigl(qz_{1},t^{-1}z_{2}\bigr)

on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
and the defect is at
z_{2}=0
, which is the set of fixed points
of
t^{-1}
-action.
2
2

2

We can regard
q
and
t
as the
\Omega
-background along the defect and transverse to the defect, respectively.

The function
\mathsf{U}(\Lambda,x)
is supposed to be a formal double power series in
x
and
\Lambda/x
:

\mathsf{U}(\Lambda,x)=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 0}c_{k,\ell}x^{k}(\Lambda/x)^{\ell},

(1.5)

where the coefficients
c_{m,n}
are functions of the
{\rm SU}(2)
Coulomb parameter
Q
, mass parameters
T_{i}

and the equivariant parameters
(q,t)
.
Physically,
\Lambda
is the instanton expansion parameter and
x
is identified with the position
of the degenerate field
\phi_{2,1}(x)
that corresponds to the defect according
to the AGT correspondence
[
4
,
5
]
.

In the case of the Virasoro algebra which corresponds to four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric gauge theories,
the degenerate field
\phi_{2,1}(x)
has a null state at level two, which implies
a differential equation of the second order in
x
as the BPZ equation.
The non-stationary difference equation (
1.3
) may be regarded as a
q
-deformed version of the BPZ equation.
However, if we try to derive it, we encounter the problem, since the natural coproduct is not known for the deformed Virasoro algebra
[
13
,
53
]
.
Hence, we cannot define intertwiners and consequently cannot
use the methods of representation theory. We can overcome such a difficulty by noticing the following fact.
Namely, the instanton partition function with a surface defect allows another description
in terms of the affine Laumon space
[
6
,
35
,
36
]
.
In this case the instanton partition function gives a conformal block of the affine Kac–Moody algebra and
its
q
deformation, the quantum affine algebra is a Hopf algebra!
In
[
10
]
(and implicitly in
[
51
]
),
it was conjectured that the instanton partition function coming from the equivariant character of
the affine Laumon space solves the gauge transformed version of (
1.3
);
See (
1.6
) below.
More concretely, in
[
10
]

we have shown that the non-stationary difference equation (
1.3
) can be factorized into a coupled system,
which is in accord with the formula of Hamiltonian of the discrete Painlevé VI equation
in terms of the extended affine Weyl group of
D_{5}^{(1)}
.
Then the conjecture is that a pair of the affine Laumon partition functions whose parameters are related
by a simple transformation provides a solution to the coupled system, see
[
10
, Conjecture 6.4]
.
In this paper, we prove this conjecture by using a connection through the
q
-KZ equation.

In order to see the relation to the
q
-KZ equation, we first make a gauge transformation
of the “wave function”
\mathsf{U}(\Lambda,x)\to\Psi(\Lambda,x)
to recast (
1.3
)
into the following form
[
10
]
:

\Psi(\Lambda,x)={\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}T_{qtQ,x}^{-1}T_{t,\Lambda}^{-1}\cdot\Psi(\Lambda,x).

(1.6)

An explicit form of the Hamiltonian
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}
is given by (
2.1
).

{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}
involves “renormalized” mass parameters
d_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq 4
which are monomials in the original mass parameters
T_{i}
,
Q
and

(q,t)
. It is amusing that we can eliminate the double infinite product
(x;q,t)_{\infty}
appeared in
\mathcal{A}_{i}(\Lambda,x)
from
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}
.
It is also remarkable that the parameters
Q
and
t
appear explicitly only through the shift parameters for
x
and
\Lambda
.
By tuning two of mass parameters, say
d_{2}=q^{-m}
and
d_{3}=q^{-n}
, the Hamiltonian acts on the
(n+m+1)
-dimensional space of
Laurent polynomials with a basis
x^{j}
,
-n\leq j\leq m
.
Then, the representation matrix of
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}
agrees with the
R
-matrix of
U_{\mathsf{v}}\bigl(A_{1}^{(1)}\bigr)
,
which allows us to identify (
1.6
) as a
q
-KZ equation. Namely, for the matrix
r_{i,j}
defined by

{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}x^{i}=\sum_{j=-n}^{m}r_{i,j}(\Lambda)x^{j},\qquad-n\leq i\leq m,

(1.7)

we have the following statement.

Theorem 1.1

(Theorem
2.3
)
.

Set
N=m+n
. The matrix
[r_{i,j}(\Lambda)]_{-n\leq i,j\leq m}
coincides with
the
(N+1)\times(N+1)
block
in the weight decomposition of the infinite-dimensional
R
-matrix for
U_{\mathsf{v}}\bigl(A_{1}^{(1)}\bigr)

for evaluation representations of generic weights.

This is one of the fundamental results in this paper.

Figure 1:
After the mass truncation
d_{2}=q^{-m}
,
d_{3}=q^{-n}
, the double series
\Psi(\Lambda,x)
becomes
a Laurent polynomial in
x
, while it is still a formal power series in
\Lambda
.
The circles represent the positions of allowed terms in the
(x,\Lambda)
-lattice.

As we mentioned above, the Jackson integral of symmetric Selberg type solves the
q
-KZ equation.
Recall that the Jackson integral is a pairing of the integration cycle
\xi
and the cocycle function
\phi(z)

\langle\phi(z),\xi\rangle=(1-t)^{N}\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}}\biggl[\phi(z)\Phi(z)\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(z_{i}-z_{j})\biggr]_{z_{i}=\xi_{i}t^{\nu_{i}}}.

(1.8)

In this paper, we take the weight function
\Phi(z)
which depends on four parameters
(a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2})
;
See Section
3
for details.
On the other hand, the instanton partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{\rm{AL}}

from the equivariant character of the affine Laumon space is defined by (
4
).
Hence, if we can show the relation of the partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{\rm{AL}}

to the Jackson integral (
1.8
), it gives a proof of the conjecture in our previous paper.
Under the same tuning conditions for mass parameters as above, the partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{\rm{AL}}

becomes a Laurent polynomial in
x
(see Figure
1
and Appendix
E
).
By carefully examining combinatorial structure of the orbifolded Nekrasov factor in (
4
) (see Appendix
F
),
we can identify the coefficients of
\mathcal{Z}_{\rm{AL}}
in the expansion parameter
x

as the Jackson integral (
1.8
) with an appropriate choice of the cycle
\xi
.
In this way, the affine Laumon partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{\rm{AL}}

agrees with the Jackson integral for the
q
-KZ equation term by term,
if we choose suitable basis of (co-)cycles.
More concretely, we have the following.

Theorem 1.2

(Theorem
4.1
)
.

Under the identification of the parameters

\displaystyle d_{1}=\frac{1}{q^{m-1}a_{1}b_{1}},\qquad d_{2}=q^{-m},\qquad d_{3}=q^{-n},\qquad d_{4}=\frac{1}{q^{n-1}a_{2}b_{2}},\qquad Q=\frac{q^{m-n}a_{1}}{ta_{2}},\!\!

(1.9)

the affine Laumon partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{\rm{AL}}
coincides with the Jackson integral (
1.8
)
with an appropriate choice of
\xi
and a suitable basis of
\phi(z)
.

A definition of the basis and the choice of
\xi
are the subjects of Section
3
.
In particular, we show that the combinatorial structure of
\mathcal{Z}_{\rm{AL}}
as a sum over the pair of Young diagrams
is in a nice correspondence with the cohomology basis called Matsuo basis in the literature.

By combining above two theorems under the specialization
d_{2}=q^{-m}
,
d_{3}=q^{-n}
,
we can show that the affine Laumon partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{\rm{AL}}

is a solution to the gauge transformed version (
1.6
) of the Shakirov’s equation.
The result can be extended to generic mass parameters
d_{i}
also by the following reasons.
We invoke the fact that the equation (
1.3
) inductively determines the expansion coefficients
c_{i,j}

in (
1.5
) uniquely up to an overall normalization, which we fix by
{c_{0,0}=1}

(see
[
10
, Proposition 2.7]
).
Moreover, from the recursion relations among
c_{i,j}
we can see that for generic mass parameters
d_{i}

they are polynomials in
d_{i}
with coefficients in the field of rational functions of
q
,
t
and
Q
.
3
3

3

If we expand the Hamiltonian
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}
in
x
and
\Lambda/x
, the leading term does not involve
d_{i}
.

Hence, if
\mathcal{Z}_{\rm{AL}}
is a solution under the specialization
d_{2}=q^{-m}
,
d_{3}=q^{-n}

for any
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, it is valid for generic values of
d_{2}
and
d_{3}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

The affine Laumon partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{\rm{AL}}
is a solution to the non-stationary difference equation
(
1.6
).

In Appendix
H
, we recast (
1.6
) to a coupled system, which is essentially the same as
the coupled system derived in
[
10
]
based on the structure of the Hamiltonian of the
qq
-Painlevé VI equation.
Hence, our main Theorem
1.3
also proves
[
10
, Conjecture 6.4]
.
Recall that the original conjecture in
[
51
]
was for the degenerate five point
q
-Virasoro conformal block,
which is realized as the Nekrasov partition function of
{\rm U}(2)\times{\rm U}(2)
linear quiver gauge
theory with the Higgsing condition on mass parameters.
One can express the partition function in question in terms of the topological vertex (intertwiners of the quantum
toroidal
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}
-algebra) and confirm that it is similarly related to the Jackson integral of the same type
as the present paper. This is regarded as a
q
-deformed analogue of
the correspondence between the current block of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
and the degenerate conformal block.
Details will be reported elsewhere.

It is known that the Jackson integral relevant for
q
-KZ equation actually satisfies two kinds of difference equations
(see, e.g.,
[
29
]
).
For the case in our hand, the second difference equation involves the shift of a “renormalized”
Coulomb modulus
(qtQ)^{-1}
of
{\rm SU}(2)
gauge theory.
4
4

4

Recall that
(qtQ)^{-1}
is the shift parameter of
x
in (
1.3
).

Two difference equations are exactly the same under the exchange

\Lambda\longleftrightarrow\frac{q^{2}}{d_{1}d_{2}}(qtQ)^{-1}.

(1.10)

In the geometric engineering of
{\rm SU}(2)
gauge theory by local
\mathbb{F}_{0}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
\Lambda
and
Q
are identified with
Kähler parameters of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Hence, this is a manifestation of the base-fiber duality in topological strings.

We will also work out the four-dimensional limit of the gauge transformed equation (
1.6
).
Then we can check that it agrees with the KZ equation, which has been derived from the regularity of the fractional fundamental

qq
-character of type
A_{1}

[
47
]
.

Since the KZ equation can be regarded as a quantization of the Schlesinger system
[
49
]
,
the relation of the conformal block of the current algebra to the quantum monodromy preserving deformation
has been believed as a folklore.
The equation (
1.6
) is quite interesting object as an explicit example realizing such correspondence.
In recent papers
[
20
,
54
]
, the quantum cohomology and (quantum) monodromy preserving deformation
has also been discussed.

It is very likely that the
q
-Borel transformation
\mathcal{B}
appears
in some representation of double affine Hecke algebras (DAHA) on polynomials
in
x_{1},x_{2},\dots

[
51
]
. From such a speculation, it is interesting
that in connection with the quantum
Q
system
[
21
,
22
]
,
an operator very close to
\mathcal{B}
is employed as Cherednik’s Gaussian operator
representing the Dehn twist on polynomial representations of spherical DAHA.

The present paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we introduce a truncation of the non-stationary difference equation (
1.3
) by tuning two of mass parameters among four.
We will call it mass truncation. After the mass truncation the Hamiltonian of (
1.3
) acts on the space of Laurent polynomials in
x
.
We find that the matrix elements
r_{i,j}(\Lambda)
satisfy
the same relation as the quantum
6j
-symbol derived by Rosengren
[
50
]
.
In Section
3
, following the work by Ito
[
29
]
, we first review the
q
-KZ equations satisfied by the Jackson integral (
1.8
),
which is a paring of an integration cycle
\xi
and a cocycle function
\varphi(z)
. We also study a special integration cycle
\xi

which reduces the bilateral sum over the lattice in the definition of the Jackson integral to a sum over a positive cone of the lattice.
This truncation (which we call lattice truncation) has been considered in literature (see, e.g.,
[
31
,
32
]
and references therein).
The relevant cycles are called the characteristic cycles or the
\alpha
-stable or
\alpha
-unstable cycles by Aomoto, and play crucial roles in the study of asymptotic behavior.
The lattice truncation is crucial, when we make an identification of the Jackson integral with the Nekrasov partition function
which is expressed as a sum over a pair of Young diagrams.
In Section
4
, we consider the Nekrasov partition function with a surface defect, which can be obtained from the equivariant character
at fixed points of the toric action on the affine Laumon space. The partition function is expanded in
x
and
\Lambda/x
and we show that
the expansion in
x
is in accord with a basis, which is called Matsuo basis, of the cocycle functions in the Jackson integral representation of
solutions to the
q
-KZ equation. In Section
5
, we compute the four-dimensional limit of the
R
-matrix that appears in the truncation
of the non-stationary difference equation and show that the limit correctly reproduces the KZ equation for
the conformal block of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
current algebra.
There are many appendices in this paper, where some helpful clarifications and technical details are presented.

We will use the following notations throughout the paper
[
28
]
:

\displaystyle\varphi(x):=(x;q)_{\infty}=\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-xq^{n})=\exp\Biggl(-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over n}{1\over 1-q^{n}}x^{n}\Biggr),\qquad|x|<1,\quad|q|<1,

(1.11)

\displaystyle(x;q,t)_{\infty}=\prod_{n,m=0}^{\infty}(1-xq^{n}t^{m})=\exp\Biggl(-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over n}{1\over(1-q^{n})(1-t^{n})}x^{n}\Biggr),

\displaystyle\hphantom{(x;q,t)_\infty=}{}|x|<1,\quad|q|,|t|<1.

(1.12)

The
q
-shifted factorial is defined by

(a;q)_{n}=\frac{(a;q)_{\infty}}{(aq^{n};q)_{\infty}}=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\bigl(1-aq^{i}\bigr).

(1.13)

It is convenient to introduce the notation

(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{k};q)_{n}:=(a_{1};q)_{n}(a_{2};q)_{n}\cdots(a_{k};q)_{n}.

(1.14)

We frequently use the short-hand notation
(a)_{n}:=(a;q)_{n}
, when there is no risk of confusion.
The following formulas are useful:

(a)_{n}=(-a)^{n}q^{n(n-1)/2}\biggl(\frac{1}{aq^{n-1}}\biggr)_{n},\qquad(a)_{k+\ell}=(a)_{k}\bigl(aq^{k}\bigr)_{\ell}.

(1.15)

The
q
-exponential function is

e_{q}(x):=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{x^{n}}{(q;q)_{n}}=\frac{1}{(x;q)_{\infty}}.

(1.16)

Finally, the
q
-binomial coefficient is defined by

\begin{bmatrix}n\\
k\end{bmatrix}_{q}:=\frac{(q;q)_{n}}{(q;q)_{k}(q;q)_{n-k}}.

(1.17)

2
Shakirov’s equation as a
\boldsymbol{q}
-KZ equation with generic spins

In
[
10
]
, we show that by an appropriate gauge transformation we can recast the original equation (
1.3
)
to the form
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}T_{qtQ,x}^{-1}T_{t,\Lambda}^{-1}\cdot\Psi(\Lambda,x)=\Psi(\Lambda,x)
with the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}

\displaystyle{}=\frac{1}{\varphi(qx)\varphi(\Lambda/x)}\cdot\mathcal{B}\cdot\frac{\varphi(\Lambda)\varphi\bigl(q^{-1}d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}d_{4}\Lambda\bigr)}{\varphi(-d_{1}x)\varphi(-d_{2}x)\varphi(-d_{3}\Lambda/x)\varphi(-d_{4}\Lambda/x)}

\displaystyle\quad{}\times\mathcal{B}\cdot\frac{1}{\varphi\bigl(q^{-1}d_{1}d_{2}x\bigr)\varphi(d_{3}d_{4}\Lambda/x)},

(2.1)

where
\varphi(z):=(z;q)_{\infty}
.
We have made the following change of variables:
5
5

5

In (
2.1
),
′
on
x
and
\Lambda
are deleted for simplicity.

\Lambda^{\prime}=tT_{2}T_{3}\Lambda,\qquad x^{\prime}=T_{2}q^{-1/2}t^{1/2}x,

(2.2)

and mass parameters:
6
6

6

We made an exchange
d_{1}\leftrightarrow d_{2}

in
[
10
, equation (5.5)]
. We also exchanged
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, which corresponds to the action of a Weyl reflection
on the Painlevé side, namely (
2.1
) is derived from
[
10
, equation (5.2)]

by the change of variables (
2.2
)
and (
2.3
).

\displaystyle d_{1}=T_{1}q^{1/2}t^{-1/2},\qquad

\displaystyle d_{2}=T_{2}^{-1}q^{1/2}t^{-1/2}Q^{-1},

\displaystyle d_{3}=T_{3}^{-1}q^{1/2}t^{-1/2},\qquad

\displaystyle d_{4}=T_{4}q^{1/2}t^{1/2}Q.

(2.3)

The Hamiltonian
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}
is manifestly symmetric under the exchange
d_{1}\leftrightarrow d_{2}
,
d_{3}\leftrightarrow d_{4}
.
Let
\vartheta_{x}:=x\partial_{x}
be the Euler operator in
x
.
We have the following commutation relations among
x,p=q^{\vartheta_{x}}
and
\mathcal{B}=q^{\vartheta_{x}(\vartheta_{x}+1)/2}
:

px=qxp,\qquad\mathcal{B}p=p\mathcal{B},\qquad\mathcal{B}x=px\mathcal{B}.

(2.4)

Lemma 2.1
.

For
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}\cdot x^{m}\mathbb{C}\biggl[\biggl[\frac{1}{x}\biggr]\biggr]\subset x^{m}\mathbb{C}\biggl[\biggl[\frac{1}{x}\biggr]\biggr]\qquad{\rm for}\ d_{1}=q^{-m}\ {\rm or}\ d_{2}=q^{-m},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}\cdot x^{-m}\mathbb{C}[[x]]\subset x^{-m}\mathbb{C}[[x]]\qquad{\rm for}\ d_{3}=q^{-m}\ {\rm or}\ d_{4}=q^{-m}.

(2.5)

Proof.

Due to the relation

{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3},d_{4},\Lambda,x)=\ q^{2}x\ {\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}(qd_{1},qd_{2},d_{3}/q,d_{4}/q,\Lambda,x)\ p^{2}x^{-1},

the statements inductively reduce to the case
m=0
.
Hence, it is enough to show the lemma for
m=0
. Note that one can omit the factors of the form

\varphi(\ast\Lambda/x)
[
\varphi(\ast x)
(resp.)] in the first [second (resp.)] relation in (
2.5
),
since they do not affect the consequence.
By omitting these factors, the relations (
2.5
) follow from the identities (
k\geq 0
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varphi(-aqx)}\mathcal{B}\frac{1}{\varphi(ax)}\cdot x^{-k}=x^{-k}\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\bigl(q^{j}+ax\bigr),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varphi(-a/x)}\mathcal{B}\frac{1}{\varphi(a/x)}\cdot x^{k}=x^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\biggl(q^{j}+\frac{a}{x}\biggr),

which are a special case of the formulas (
B.1
) in Appendix
B
.
∎

Due to Lemma
2.1
, we have the following action of
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}
with finite support:

{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}x^{i}=\sum_{j=-n}^{m}r_{i,j}(\Lambda)x^{j},\qquad-n\leq i\leq m,

(2.6)

when the parameters are specialized as
d_{2}=q^{-m}
,
d_{3}=q^{-n}
,
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
7
7

7

Due to the symmetry
d_{1}\leftrightarrow d_{2}
,
d_{3}\leftrightarrow d_{4}
, the cases
d_{1}=q^{-m}
,
d_{4}=q^{-n}
etc.
have the similar truncation.

The coefficient
r_{i,j}(\Lambda)
is a rational function in the spectral parameter
\Lambda
.
It also depends on the remaining mass parameters
d_{1}
and
d_{4}
, which are to be identified with
the highest weights of the in-state and the out-state, when we consider a matrix element (two point function)
of the intertwiners.

Example 2.2
.

For
(m,n)=(1,0)
,
(2,0)
, we have

\begin{bmatrix}r_{0,0}&r_{0,1}\\
r_{1,0}&r_{1,1}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{(\frac{d_{1}\Lambda}{q})_{1}}{(\frac{\Lambda}{q})_{1}}&-\frac{(d_{1})_{1}}{(\frac{\Lambda}{q})_{1}}\vskip 2.84526pt\\
-\frac{\Lambda q(\frac{d_{4}}{q})_{1}}{(\frac{\Lambda}{q})_{1}}&\frac{q^{2}(\frac{d_{4}\Lambda}{q^{2}})_{1}}{(\frac{\Lambda}{q})_{1}}\\
\end{bmatrix}

(2.7)

and

\begin{bmatrix}r_{0,0}&r_{0,1}&r_{0,2}\\
r_{1,0}&r_{1,1}&r_{1,2}\\
r_{2,0}&r_{2,1}&r_{2,2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{(\frac{d_{1}\Lambda}{q^{2}})_{1}(\frac{d_{1}\Lambda}{q})_{1}}{(\frac{\Lambda}{q^{2}})_{1}(\frac{\Lambda}{q})_{1}}&-\frac{(1+q)(d_{1})_{1}(\frac{d_{1}\Lambda}{q})_{1}}{q(\frac{\Lambda}{q^{2}})_{1}(\frac{\Lambda}{q})_{1}}&\frac{(d_{1})_{1}(d_{1}q)_{1}}{q(\frac{\Lambda}{q^{2}})_{1}(\frac{\Lambda}{q})_{1}}\vskip 2.84526pt\\
-\frac{\Lambda q(\frac{d_{4}}{q})_{1}(\frac{d_{1}\Lambda}{q})_{1}}{(\frac{\Lambda}{q^{2}})_{1}(\frac{\Lambda}{q})_{1}}&\frac{q^{2}(\frac{d_{1}\Lambda}{q})_{1}(\frac{d_{4}\Lambda}{q^{2}})_{1}+\Lambda q(d_{1}q)_{1}(\frac{d_{4}}{q^{2}})_{1}}{(\frac{\Lambda}{q^{2}})_{1}(\frac{\Lambda}{q})_{1}}&-\frac{q^{2}(d_{1}q)_{1}(\frac{d_{4}\Lambda}{q^{3}})_{1}}{(\frac{\Lambda}{q^{2}})_{1}(\frac{\Lambda}{q})_{1}}\vskip 2.84526pt\\
\frac{\Lambda^{2}q^{3}(\frac{d_{4}}{q^{2}})_{1}(\frac{d_{4}}{q})_{1}}{(\frac{\Lambda}{q^{2}})_{1}(\frac{\Lambda}{q})_{1}}&-\frac{\Lambda q^{4}(1+q)(\frac{d_{4}}{q^{2}})_{1}(\frac{d_{4}\Lambda}{q^{3}})_{1}}{(\frac{\Lambda}{q^{2}})_{1}(\frac{\Lambda}{q})_{1}}&\frac{q^{6}(\frac{d_{4}\Lambda}{q^{4}})_{1}(\frac{d_{4}\Lambda}{q^{3}})_{1}}{(\frac{\Lambda}{q^{2}})_{1}(\frac{\Lambda}{q})_{1}}\end{bmatrix},

(2.8)

where
r_{i,j}=r_{i,j}(\Lambda)
,
(x)_{1}=(1-x)
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Set
N=m+n
. The matrix
[r_{i,j}(\Lambda)]_{-n\leq i,j\leq m}
coincides with
the
(N+1)\times(N+1)
block
in the weight decomposition of the infinite-dimensional
R
-matrix for
U_{\mathsf{v}}\bigl(A_{1}^{(1)}\bigr)

for evaluation representations of generic weights
[
14
,
38
]
.
8
8

8

See Remark
2.4
below and computations in Section
2.1
for more precise identification.

Proof.

The defining relation (
2.6
) of
r_{i,j}(\Lambda)
can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi(\Lambda)\varphi\bigl(q^{-1}d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}d_{4}\Lambda\bigr)}{\varphi(-d_{1}x)\varphi(-d_{2}x)\varphi(-d_{3}\Lambda/x)\varphi(-d_{4}\Lambda/x)}\mathcal{B}\frac{1}{\varphi(q^{-1}d_{1}d_{2}x)\varphi(d_{3}d_{4}\Lambda/x)}x^{i}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\mathcal{B}^{-1}\varphi(qx)\varphi\biggl(\frac{\Lambda}{x}\biggr)\sum_{j=-n}^{m}r_{i,j}(\Lambda)x^{j}.

(2.9)

Using the relations (see Appendix
B
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\frac{1}{\varphi\bigl(q^{-1}d_{1}d_{2}x\bigr)\varphi(d_{3}d_{4}\frac{\Lambda}{x})}x^{i}=\frac{\varphi\bigl(-q^{i}d_{1}d_{2}x\bigr)\varphi\bigl(-q^{-i}d_{3}d_{4}\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}{\varphi\bigl(q^{-1}d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}d_{4}\Lambda\bigr)}q^{i(i+1)/2}x^{i},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{-1}\varphi(qx)\varphi\biggl(\frac{\Lambda}{x}\biggr)x^{j}=q^{-j(j+1)/2}x^{j}\frac{\varphi(\Lambda)}{\varphi\bigl(-q^{-j}x\bigr)\varphi\bigl(-q^{j}\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)},

(2.10)

we can rewrite (
2.9
) as

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi\bigl(-q^{i}d_{1}d_{2}x\bigr)\varphi\bigl(-q^{-i}d_{3}d_{4}\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}{\varphi(-d_{3}\Lambda/x)\varphi(-d_{4}\Lambda/x)}q^{i(i+1)/2}x^{i}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{j=-n}^{m}r_{i,j}(\Lambda)q^{-j(j+1)/2}x^{j}\frac{\varphi(-d_{1}x)\varphi(-d_{2}x)}{\varphi\bigl(-q^{-j}x\bigr)\varphi\bigl(-q^{j}\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}.

(2.11)

Since
d_{2}=q^{-m}
,
d_{3}=q^{-n}
,
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
this equation can be simplified to

\displaystyle q^{\frac{1}{2}i(i+1)}x^{i}\bigl(-d_{1}q^{i-m}x\bigr)_{m-i}\biggl(-d_{4}q^{-i-n}\frac{\Lambda}{x}\biggr)_{i+n}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{j=-n}^{m}r_{i,j}(\Lambda)q^{-\frac{1}{2}j(j+1)}x^{j}(-q^{-m}x)_{m-j}\biggl(-q^{-n}\frac{\Lambda}{x}\biggr)_{j+n},

(2.12)

where
(a)_{n}=(a;q)_{n}
.
This relation is essentially the same as the equation for the
q
-
6j
symbols
[
50
]
.
Hence, the coefficients
r_{i,j}(\Lambda)
is given by the
q
-
6j
(i.e.,
R
-matrix) for
U_{\mathsf{v}}\bigl(A_{1}^{(1)}\bigr)
.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

Some explicit formulas for
U_{\mathsf{v}}\bigl(A_{1}^{(1)}\bigr)

R
-matrix are known
[
14
,
38
]
.
One of such expressions is related to the hypergeometric series
{}_{4}\phi_{3}

with the base
q
,
9
9

9

The parameter
q
in
[
14
,
38
]

is denoted by
{\sf v}=q^{\frac{1}{2}}
in this paper.

R^{\rm HG}_{i,j}=\beta^{-j}\frac{(q)_{N}\bigl(\frac{\alpha}{z}\bigr)_{N-i}\bigl(\frac{1}{\beta}\bigr)_{N-j}\bigl(\frac{\beta}{z}\bigr)_{j}}{(q)_{j}\bigl(q\bigr)_{N-j}\bigl(\frac{1}{z}\bigr)_{N}\bigl(\frac{1}{\beta}\bigr)_{N-i}}\sum_{k=0}^{j}\frac{\bigl(q^{-j}\bigr)_{k}\bigl(q^{i-N}\bigr)_{k}\bigl(q^{1-N}z\bigr)_{k}\bigl(\frac{z}{\alpha\beta}\bigr)_{k}}{(q)_{k}\bigl(q^{-N}\bigr)_{k}\bigl(\frac{q^{1+i-N}z}{\alpha}\bigr)_{k}\bigl(\frac{q^{1-j}z}{\beta}\bigr)_{k}}q^{k}.

(2.13)

For
d_{2}=q^{-m}
,
d_{3}=q^{-n}
and
i,j\in[-n,m]
, one can show (see Section
2.1
)

\begin{array}[]{l}r_{i,j}(\Lambda)=d_{1}^{m-i}q^{(m+1)i}R^{\rm HG}_{i+n,j+n}\big|_{\{N=m+n,z=\frac{\Lambda}{q},\alpha=\frac{q^{n}}{d_{1}},\beta=\frac{q^{m}}{d_{4}}\}},\end{array}

(2.14)

hence, the matrices
\{r_{i,j}(\Lambda)\}_{i,j\in[-n,m]}
with fixed
N=m+n
give essentially the same
(N+1)\times(N+1)

R
-matrix.

The above result implies that the equation

\Psi(\Lambda,x)={\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}\Psi\biggl(\frac{\Lambda}{t},\frac{x}{qtQ}\biggr),

(2.15)

for
d_{2}=q^{-m}
,
d_{3}=q^{-n}
,
N=m+n
, is written as the
t
-difference equation with
(N+1)\times(N+1)
matrix coefficient:

\psi_{j}(\Lambda)=\sum_{i=-n}^{m}\psi_{i}\biggl(\frac{\Lambda}{t}\biggr)r_{i,j}(\Lambda)(qtQ)^{-i},

(2.16)

where
\Psi(\Lambda,x)=\sum_{i=-n}^{m}x^{i}\psi_{i}(\Lambda)
.
This is exactly the
q
-KZ equation for the four point function of
U_{\mathsf{v}}\bigl(A_{1}^{(1)}\bigr)

in
N
-down spin sector.
10
10

10

The parameter
Q
specifies the intermediate state of the correlation
function
\langle j_{4}|\Phi_{j_{3}}(1)\Phi_{j_{2}}(\Lambda)|j_{1}\rangle
.
The variable
x
is merely a book-keeping parameter for the spin direction.

The cases
(m,n)
with
m+n=N
correspond to an equivalent equation of size
N+1
.
In Section
4
, we will show that the specializations of the affine Laumon partition function
corresponding to the condition
d_{2}=q^{-m}
,
d_{3}=q^{-n}
give a set of
N+1
solutions which form the fundamental solutions.
In this sense, the Laumon partition function can be viewed as the “universal fundamental solution” for
q
-KZ equation.

2.1
Computation of
\boldsymbol{r_{i,j}}

We will explain how to compute
(r_{i,j})_{i,j=-n}^{m}
from (
2.12
).
By using the formula (
1.15
), the relation (
2.12
) can be written as

q^{-in}(\Lambda d_{4})^{i+n}U_{i}(x)=\sum_{j=-n}^{m}r_{i,j}q^{-j}\Lambda^{j+n}V_{j}(x),

(2.17)

where

U_{i}(x)=\bigl(-d_{1}q^{i-m}x\bigr)_{m-i}\biggl(-\frac{qx}{\Lambda d_{4}}\biggr)_{i+n},\qquad V_{j}(x)=(-q^{-m}x)_{m-j}\biggl(-\frac{q^{1-j}x}{\Lambda}\biggr)_{j+n}.

(2.18)

Since both
\{U_{i}(x)\}_{i=-n}^{m}
and
\{V_{j}(x)\}_{j=-n}^{m}
are bases of polynomials of degree
m+n
,
our task is to compute the transition matrix between these two bases. For this purpose it is convenient to introduce an intermediate basis
\{W_{k}(x)\}_{k=-n}^{m}
defined by
11
11

11

Another choice

\tilde{W}_{k}(x)=U_{k}(x){|}_{d_{4}\mapsto\frac{q^{m+1}}{\Lambda}}
also works as well,
which gives different decomposition of the same matrix
(r_{i,j})
.

W_{k}(x)=U_{k}(x)\Big|_{d_{1}\mapsto\frac{q^{n+1}}{\Lambda}}=\biggl(-\frac{q^{n+1+k-m}}{\Lambda}x\biggr)_{m-k}\biggl(-\frac{qx}{\Lambda d_{4}}\biggr)_{k+n}.

(2.19)

The polynomial
W_{k}(x)
is defined so that the zeros of
U_{i}(x)
,
W_{k}(x)
,
V_{j}(x)
overlap with each other as follows:

\displaystyle U_{i}(x)\colon\ -x=\underbrace{\frac{q}{d_{1}},\dots,\frac{q}{d_{1}}q^{m-i-1}}_{m-i},\,\underbrace{\frac{d_{4}\Lambda}{q^{n+i}},\dots,\frac{d_{4}\Lambda}{q}}_{n+i},

\displaystyle W_{k}(x)\colon\ -x=\underbrace{\frac{\Lambda}{q^{n}},\dots,\frac{\Lambda}{q^{n}}q^{m-k-1}}_{m-k},\,\underbrace{\frac{d_{4}\Lambda}{q^{n+k}},\dots,\frac{d_{4}\Lambda}{q}}_{n+k},

\displaystyle V_{j}(x)\colon\ -x=\underbrace{\frac{\Lambda}{q^{n}},\dots,\frac{\Lambda}{q^{n}}q^{n+j-1}}_{n+j},\,\underbrace{q^{j+1},\dots,q^{m}}_{m-j}.

(2.20)

Let us compute the transition coefficients
r^{UW}_{i,k}
and
r^{WV}_{k,j}
defined by

U_{i}(x)=\sum_{k=-n}^{m}r^{UW}_{i,k}W_{k}(x),\qquad W_{k}(x)=\sum_{j=-n}^{m}r^{WV}_{k,j}V_{j}(x).

(2.21)

Due to the cancellation of common factors arising from (
2.20
), the first relation of (
2.21
) can be written as

\displaystyle\bigl(-d_{1}q^{i-m}x\bigr)_{m-i}=\sum_{k=i}^{m}r^{UW}_{i,k}\biggl(-\frac{q^{n+1+k-m}}{\Lambda}x\biggr)_{m-k}\biggl(-\frac{q^{i+n+1}x}{\Lambda d_{4}}\biggr)_{k-i}.

(2.22)

Note that we may delete the coefficient of
W_{k}(x)
which does not have the common zeros with
U_{i}(x)
.
Similarly, from the second equation of (
2.21
), we have

\displaystyle\biggl(-\frac{qx}{\Lambda d_{4}}\biggr)_{k+n}=\sum_{j=m-k-n}^{m}r^{WV}_{k,j}(-q^{-m}x)_{m-j}\biggl(-\frac{q^{1-j}x}{\Lambda}\biggr)_{j+n-m+k}.

(2.23)

Proposition 2.5
.

The nonzero coefficients of

r^{UW}_{i,k}
,
i\leq k
, can be determined as

\displaystyle r^{UW}_{i,k}=q^{\frac{1}{2}(i-k)(2m+1+i-k)}(-d_{4})^{k-i}{\displaystyle\frac{(q)_{m-i}}{(q)_{k-i}(q)_{m-k}}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{r^{UW}_{i,k}=}{}\times{\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(d_{1}\Lambda q^{i-k-n}\bigr)_{k-i}\bigl(d_{1}d_{4}\Lambda q^{-m-n-1}\bigr)_{m-k}}{(d_{4}q^{-m})_{m-i}}}.

(2.24)

Hence,
\bigl(r^{UW}_{i,k}\bigr)
is upper triangular matrix with factorized elements.
Similarly, the nonvanishing coefficients of
r^{WU}_{k,j}
,
N-(k+n)\leq j+n
, are

\displaystyle r^{WV}_{k,j}=q^{\frac{1}{2}(j-k-m-n-1)(j+k-m+n)}(-\Lambda)^{j+k-m+n}{\displaystyle\frac{(q)_{k+n}}{(q)_{k+j-m+n}(q)_{m-j}}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{r^{WV}_{k,j}=}{}\times{}{\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(\frac{q^{m+1}}{d_{4}\Lambda}\bigr)_{k+j-m+n}\bigl(\frac{q^{j+1}}{d_{4}}\bigr)_{m-j}}{\bigl(q^{-k-n}\Lambda\bigr)_{k+n}}}.

(2.25)

The matrix
\bigl(r^{WV}_{k,j}\bigr)
is “lower triangular along anti-diagonal” with factorized elements.

Proof.

We follow the method in
[
50
, Section 3]
.
By the change of the parameters

N=m-i,\qquad r=m-k,\qquad(a,b,c)=\biggl(-d_{1}q^{i-m},-\frac{q^{i+n+1}}{d_{4}\Lambda},-\frac{q^{n+1}}{\Lambda}\biggr)

(2.26)

for (
2.22
), and

N=k+n,\qquad r=j+k-m+n,\qquad(a,b,c)=\biggl(-\frac{q}{d_{4}\Lambda},-q^{-m},-\frac{q^{k-m+n+1}}{\Lambda}\biggr)

(2.27)

for (
2.23
), respectively, both equations are written as the binomial expansion

(ax)_{N}=\sum_{r=0}^{N}C_{N,r}(bx)_{N-r}(cq^{-r}x)_{r}.

(2.28)

This implies that the coefficients
C_{N,r}
are given by

C_{N,r}=q^{r(r+1)/2}\biggl(-\frac{b}{c}\biggr)^{r}\frac{(q)_{N}}{(q)_{r}(q)_{N-r}}\frac{\bigl(\frac{a}{b}\bigr)_{r}\bigl(q^{r+1}\frac{a}{c}\bigr)_{N-r}}{\bigl(\frac{bq}{c}\bigr)_{N}}.

(2.29)

To see this, note that

\displaystyle 1-q^{N}ax=A_{r}\bigl(1-q^{N-r}bx\bigr)+B_{r}\bigl(1-q^{-r-1}cx\bigr),

\displaystyle A_{r}={\displaystyle\frac{c-aq^{N+1+r}}{c-bq^{N+1}}},\qquad B_{r}={\displaystyle\frac{b-aq^{r}}{b-cq^{-N-1}}}.

(2.30)

Hence,

\frac{(ax)_{N+1}}{(ax)_{N}}=A_{r}\frac{(bx)_{N+1-r}}{(bx)_{N-r}}+B_{r}\frac{\bigl(cq^{-r-1}x\bigr)_{r+1}}{(cq^{-r}x)_{r}}.

(2.31)

From this, the coefficients
C_{N,r}
are uniquely determined by “Pascal’s triangle”

C_{N+1,r}=A_{r}C_{N,r}+B_{r-1}C_{N,r-1},

(2.32)

with the boundary conditions

C_{0,0}=1
,
C_{N,-1}=C_{N,N+1}=0
.
For
C_{N,r}
given by (
2.29
), the boundary conditions are obvious.
The relation (
2.32
) follows from

\frac{C_{N+1,r}}{C_{N,r}}=\frac{\bigl(1-q^{N+1}\bigr)\bigl(1-\frac{aq^{N+1}}{c}\bigr)}{\bigl(1-q^{N+1-r}\bigr)\bigl(1-\frac{bq^{N+1}}{c}\bigr)},\qquad\frac{C_{N,r-1}}{C_{N,r}}=-\frac{cq^{-r}(1-q^{r})\bigl(1-\frac{aq^{r}}{c}\bigr)}{b\bigl(1-q^{N+1-r}\bigr)\bigl(1-\frac{aq^{r-1}}{b}\bigr)}

(2.33)

and the identity

\displaystyle\bigl(1-q^{N+1}\bigr)\biggl(1-\frac{a}{c}q^{N+1}\biggr)-q^{N+1-r}(1-q^{r})\biggl(1-\frac{a}{c}q^{r}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\bigl(1-q^{N+1-r}\bigr)\biggl(1-\frac{a}{c}q^{N+1+r}\biggr)=0.

(2.34)

Hence, (
2.29
) is proved.
Back to the original parameters, we obtain the desired results.
∎

Combining (
2.24
) and (
2.25
), we have

\displaystyle r_{i,j}=q^{-in}(\Lambda d_{4})^{i+n}q^{j}\Lambda^{-j-n}r^{UV}_{i,j}=q^{-in}(\Lambda d_{4})^{i+n}q^{j}\Lambda^{-j-n}\sum_{k=-n}^{m}r^{UW}_{i,k}r^{WV}_{k,j}

\displaystyle\hphantom{r_{i,j}}{}=\sum_{k=m-n-j}^{m}q^{-in}q^{j}q^{\frac{1}{2}(i-k)(2m+1+i-k)}q^{\frac{1}{2}(j-k-m-n-1)(j+k-m+n)}(-1)^{-m+n-i+j}

\displaystyle\hphantom{r_{i,j}=}{}\times d_{4}^{k+n}\Lambda^{i+n+k-m}{\displaystyle\frac{(q)_{m-i}}{(q)_{k-i}(q)_{m-k}}}{\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(d_{1}\Lambda q^{i-k-n}\bigr)_{k-i}\bigl(d_{1}d_{4}\Lambda q^{-m-n-1}\bigr)_{m-k}}{(d_{4}q^{-m})_{m-i}}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{r_{i,j}=}{}\times{\displaystyle\frac{(q)_{k+n}}{(q)_{k+j-m+n}(q)_{m-j}}}{\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(\frac{q^{m+1}}{d_{4}\Lambda}\bigr)_{k+j-m+n}\bigl(\frac{q^{j+1}}{d_{4}}\bigr)_{m-j}}{(q^{-k-n}\Lambda)_{k+n}}}.

(2.35)

To show (
2.14
), we make the change of variables

\displaystyle z=\frac{\Lambda}{q},\qquad\alpha=\frac{q^{n}}{d_{1}},\qquad\beta=\frac{q^{m}}{d_{4}}.

(2.36)

Using
(a)_{k}=(-a)^{k}q^{k(k-1)/2}\bigl(\frac{1}{aq^{k-1}}\bigr)_{k}
and
(a)_{k+l}=(a)_{k}\bigl(aq^{k}\bigr)_{l}
,
we compute

\displaystyle r_{i,j}=d_{1}^{m-i}\beta^{-n-j}q^{(m+1)i}{\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(\frac{\alpha}{z}\bigr)_{m-i}\bigl(\frac{1}{\beta}\bigr)_{m-j}\bigl(\frac{\beta}{z}\bigr)_{j+n}}{\bigl(\frac{1}{z}\bigr)_{m+n}\bigl(\frac{1}{\beta}\bigr)_{m-i}}}\sum_{k=m-n-j}^{m}q^{(m-k)(i+n)}

\displaystyle\hphantom{r_{i,j} =}{}\times\biggl(\frac{\beta}{z}\biggr)^{m-k}\!{\displaystyle\frac{(q)_{m-i}}{(q)_{k-i}(q)_{m-k}}}{\displaystyle\frac{(q)_{k+n}}{(q)_{k+j-m+n}(q)_{m-j}}}{\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(\frac{z}{\alpha\beta}\bigr)_{m-k}}{\bigl(\frac{z}{\alpha}q^{1+i-m}\bigr)_{m-k}}}{\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(zq^{1-N}\bigr)_{m-k}}{\bigl(\frac{\beta}{z}q^{j+n+k-m}\bigr)_{m-k}}}.\!\!\!\!

(2.37)

One can check these coefficients
r_{i,j}
coincide with the results in Remark
2.4
.

3
Jackson integral representation of solutions

to the
\boldsymbol{q}
-KZ equation

3.1
Jackson integral of symmetric Selberg type

Solutions to the
q
-KZ equation allow a representation by the Jackson integral. In this subsection we
review the results of
[
29
]
which are needed later.

In the construction of solutions to the
q
-KZ equation for
U_{\mathsf{v}}\bigl(A_{1}^{(1)}\bigr)
by M. Ito
[
29
]
,
the
N
-tuple Jackson integral with the integration variables
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})

\langle\phi(z),\xi\rangle=(1-t)^{N}\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}}[\phi(z)\Phi(z)\Delta(1,z)]_{z_{i}=\xi_{i}t^{\nu_{i}}}

(3.1)

is considered.
12
12

12

In view of the application in the next section, we exchange
q
and
t
in the original paper
[
29
]
.

Here,

\Delta(q,z):=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\bigl(z_{i}-q^{-1}z_{j}\bigr),

(3.2)

and

\Phi(z)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}z_{i}^{\alpha}{\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(ta_{1}^{-1}z_{i},ta_{2}^{-1}z_{i};t\bigr)_{\infty}}{(b_{1}z_{i},b_{2}z_{i};t)_{\infty}}}\cdot\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}z_{i}^{2(\log_{t}q)-1}\frac{\bigl(tq^{-1}z_{j}/z_{i};t\bigr)_{\infty}}{(qz_{j}/z_{i};t)_{\infty}}

(3.3)

is a common weight factor with four parameters
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
,
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
.
Note that
\Delta(1,z)
is nothing but the Vandermonde determinant.
In the pairing (
3.1
),
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{N})
has the meaning of parameters for an integration cycle.
On the other hand,
\phi(z)
defines a cocycle function.
In order to give a basis of the space of cohomology classes, in
[
29
]
the symmetric polynomial

\tilde{E}_{k,i}(a,b;z)={\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Delta(1,z)}}{{\mathcal{A}}(E_{k,i}(a,b;z))}={\mathcal{S}}{\displaystyle\frac{E_{k,i}(a,b;z)}{\Delta(1,z)}},\qquad a,b\in\mathbb{C}^{\times},

(3.4)

was employed by introducing

E_{k,i}(a,b;z)=z_{1}\cdots z_{k}~\Delta(q,z)\prod_{j=1}^{N-i}(1-bz_{j})\prod_{j=N-i+1}^{N}(1-a^{-1}z_{j}).

(3.5)

Here
{\mathcal{S}}
/
{\mathcal{A}}
denotes the symmetrization/anti-symmetrization of the variables
(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})
, respectively.
In particular,
e_{i}(a,b;z)=\tilde{E}_{0,i}(a,b;z)
,
0\leq i\leq N
, gives a basis which is called Matsuo basis
[
39
,
40
]
(see also
[
41
,
55
]
).

The function
c(x)=x^{\log_{t}(\frac{a}{b})}\frac{\vartheta(ax;t)}{\vartheta(bx;t)}
with

\vartheta(x;t)=(x;t)_{\infty}(t/x;t)_{\infty}
satisfies
c(tx)=c(x)
. Such a function is called pseudo constant.
The following lemma shows that the function
\Phi(z)\Delta(1,z)
in (
3.1
)
can be written in a symmetric form, up to some pseudo constant factor.
Due to this property, the integral is sometimes called Jackson integral of symmetric Selberg type.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\tau=\log_{t}q
and
i,j\in[N]:=\{1,2,\dots,N\}
. We have

\prod_{i\neq j}\frac{\bigl(tq^{-1}z_{j}/z_{i};t\bigr)_{\infty}}{(tz_{j}/z_{i};t)_{\infty}}=C(z)\Delta(1,z)\prod_{i=1}^{N}z_{i}^{-\tau(N-1)}\prod_{i<j}z_{i}^{2\tau-1}\frac{\bigl(tq^{-1}z_{j}/z_{i};t\bigr)_{\infty}}{(qz_{j}/z_{i};t)_{\infty}},

(3.6)

where

C(z)=\prod_{i<j}\bigl(\frac{z_{j}}{z_{i}}\bigr)^{\tau}\frac{\vartheta(tq^{-1}z_{i}/z_{j};t)}{\vartheta(tz_{i}/z_{j};t)}

is a pseudo constant for each variable
z_{i}
.

Proof.

The left-hand side is computed as follows:

\displaystyle\prod_{i\neq j}\frac{\bigl(tq^{-1}z_{j}/z_{i};t\bigr)_{\infty}}{(tz_{j}/z_{i};t)_{\infty}}=

\displaystyle~\prod_{i<j}\frac{\bigl(tq^{-1}z_{i}/z_{j};t\bigr)_{\infty}(tq^{-1}z_{j}/z_{i};t)_{\infty}}{(tz_{i}/z_{j};t)_{\infty}(tz_{j}/z_{i};t)_{\infty}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\prod_{i<j}\frac{\vartheta\bigl(tq^{-1}z_{i}/z_{j};t\bigr)(z_{j}/z_{i};t)_{\infty}\bigl(tq^{-1}z_{j}/z_{i};t\bigr)_{\infty}}{\vartheta(tz_{i}/z_{j};t)(qz_{j}/z_{i})_{\infty}(tz_{j}/z_{i};t)_{\infty}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~C(z)\prod_{i<j}\Bigl(\frac{z_{j}}{z_{i}}\Bigr)^{-\tau}(1-z_{j}/z_{i})\frac{\bigl(tq^{-1}z_{j}/z_{i};t\bigr)_{\infty}}{(qz_{j}/z_{i};t)_{\infty}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~C(z)\Delta(1,z)\prod_{i=1}^{N}z_{i}^{-\tau(N-1)}\prod_{i<j}z_{i}^{2\tau-1}\frac{\bigl(tq^{-1}z_{j}/z_{i};t\bigr)_{\infty}}{(qz_{j}/z_{i};t)_{\infty}}.

The last line follows from

\prod_{i<j}\Bigl(\frac{z_{j}}{z_{i}}\Bigr)^{-\tau}z_{i}^{-1}=\prod_{i<j}(z_{j}z_{i})^{-\tau}z_{i}^{2\tau-1}=\prod_{i=1}^{N}z_{i}^{-\tau(N-1)}\prod_{i<j}z_{i}^{2\tau-1}.

For the cocycle function
\phi(z)
in the pairing (
3.1
),
we will take the Matsuo basis
e_{0}(a_{2},b_{1}),\dots,\allowbreak e_{N}(a_{2},b_{1})
.
13
13

13

Since the common factor (
3.3
) is
symmetric under the exchanges
a_{1}\leftrightarrow a_{2}
and
b_{1}\leftrightarrow b_{2}
, there are four choices of the Matsuo basis.
Here we follow the choice in
[
29
]
.

Namely, we consider the following
\mathbb{C}^{N+1}
-valued function

\Psi=[\Psi_{-n},\dots,\Psi_{m}]=[\langle e_{N}(a_{2},b_{1}),\xi\rangle,\dots,\langle e_{0}(a_{2},b_{1}),\xi\rangle].

(3.7)

In order to identify the instanton partition function to be introduced in the next section with the Jackson integral,
it is important to keep the factorized structure of the integrand as far as possible,
hence we use the following expression:

\displaystyle{\hat{e}}_{k}(a,b;z)

\displaystyle{}=e_{N-k}(a,b;z)

(3.8)

\displaystyle{}=[k]_{q^{-1}}![N-k]_{q^{-1}}!\sum_{I\sqcup J=[N],|J|=k}\prod_{i\in I}\biggl(1-\frac{z_{i}}{a}\biggr)\prod_{j\in J}(1-bz_{j})\prod_{i\in I,j\in J}\frac{z_{j}-q^{-1}z_{i}}{z_{j}-z_{i}},

where the sum is taken over the
\bigl({N\atop k}\bigr)
terms corresponding
to disjoint union
I\sqcup J=[N]=\{1,2,\dots,N\}
with
|I|=N-k
,
|J|=k
.
The formula (
3.8
) follows from Proposition
G.1
in Appendix
G
for the case
s=2

with
f_{1}(x)=1-b_{1}x
and
f_{2}(x)=1-\frac{x}{a_{2}}
.
14
14

14

As noted in
[
48
]
,
these cocycle functions naturally arise as the Bethe vector.
See
[
1
]
for the developments based on the geometric method.

Thus the summation in Jackson integral is written as the sum over the cone (
3.22
) and
additional
2^{N}=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\bigl({N\atop k}\bigr)
sums. As we will see in the next section,
in the relevant instanton partition function, the first sum corresponds to the sum over the two Young diagrams
with even lengths for all the columns and the second sum comes from their even/odd variants depending on the number of
columns with odd length.

The expression (
3.8
) looks like rational, but it is a polynomial in
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})
.
In fact, the base
e_{k}(a,b;z)
can be characterized as the linear combination of the elementary symmetric functions in

z
having the following specialization
[
29
,
32
]
:

e_{k}\bigl(a,b;\bigl(x,xq\dots,xq^{N-1}\bigr)\bigr)=[N]_{q^{-1}}!\prod_{i=1}^{N-k}\bigl(1-q^{i}bx\bigr)\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\biggl(1-q^{i}\frac{x}{a}\biggr).

(3.9)

In
[
29
]
, the following fact is proved.

Theorem 3.2

(
[
29
]
)
.

With respect to the shifts
T_{\alpha}\colon\alpha\to\alpha+1
and
T_{i}=T_{t,b_{i}}^{-1}T_{t,a_{i}}
,
i=1,2
,
the Jackson integral
\Psi
satisfies the following system of difference equations:

\displaystyle T_{\alpha}\Psi=\Psi K_{0},\qquad T_{1}\Psi=\Psi K_{1},\qquad T_{2}\Psi=\Psi K_{2},

(3.10)

where

\displaystyle K_{0}=R^{-1}AR=D_{2}AD_{2}^{-1},\qquad K_{1}=R^{-1}D_{1},\qquad K_{2}=D_{2}(T_{2}R),

\displaystyle D_{1}=\bigl(\bigl(t^{\alpha}q^{N-1}\bigr)^{N-i}\delta_{ij}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq N},\qquad D_{2}=\bigl(\bigl(t^{\alpha}q^{N-1}\bigr)^{i}\delta_{ij}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq N},

(3.11)

and
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker symbol.
An explicit form of the matrix
R
is given by
15
15

15

The matrix
R
in this section is slightly different from the
R
matrix in the previous section.
See Appendix
C
for a summary of various
R
-like matrices used in this paper.

\displaystyle R=~L_{R}D_{R}U_{R},

\displaystyle L_{R}=\bigl(l^{R}_{ij}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq N},\qquad D_{R}=\bigl(\delta_{ij}d^{R}_{j}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq N},\qquad U_{R}=\bigl(u^{R}_{ij}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq N},

(3.12)

where

\displaystyle l^{R}_{ij}=\begin{bmatrix}N-j\\
N-i\end{bmatrix}_{q^{-1}}{\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{i-j}q^{-\left({i-j\atop 2}\right)}(a_{2}b_{2}q^{j};q)_{i-j}}{\bigl(a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}q^{-(N-2j-1)};q\bigr)_{i-j}}},\qquad N\geq i\geq j\geq 0,

\displaystyle d^{R}_{j}={\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(a_{1}a_{2}^{-1}q^{-j};q\bigr)_{N-j}(a_{2}b_{1};q)_{j}}{(a_{1}b_{2};q)_{N-j}\bigl(a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}q^{-(N-j)};q\bigr)_{j}}},\qquad 0\leq j\leq N,

\displaystyle u^{R}_{ij}=\begin{bmatrix}j\\
i\end{bmatrix}_{q^{-1}}{\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(a_{1}b_{1}q^{N-j};q\bigr)_{j-i}}{\bigl(a_{1}a_{2}^{-1}q^{N-i-j};q\bigr)_{j-i}}},\qquad 0\leq i\leq j\leq N,

(3.13)

and other elements are zero.
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}n\\
k\end{smallmatrix}\right]_{q^{-1}}
denotes
q^{-1}
-binomial coefficient
(
see (
1.17
)
)
.
The matrix
A
is also given in a Gauss decomposed form as

\displaystyle A=L_{A}D_{A}U_{A},

\displaystyle L_{A}=\bigl(l^{A}_{ij}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq N},\qquad D_{A}=\bigl(\delta_{ij}d^{A}_{j}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq N},\qquad U_{A}=\bigl(u^{A}_{ij}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq N},

(3.14)

where the nonzero elements are

\displaystyle l^{A}_{ij}=(-1)^{i-j}q^{\left({N-i\atop 2}\right)-\left({N-j\atop 2}\right)}\begin{bmatrix}N-j\\
N-i\end{bmatrix}_{q}{\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(a_{2}b_{2}q^{j};q\bigr)_{i-j}}{\bigl(t^{\alpha}a_{2}b_{2}q^{2j};q\bigr)_{i-j}}},\qquad N\geq i\geq j\geq 0,

\displaystyle d^{A}_{j}=a_{1}^{N-j}a_{2}^{j}q^{\left({j\atop 2}\right)+\left({N-j\atop 2}\right)}{\displaystyle\frac{(t^{\alpha};q)_{j}\bigl(t^{\alpha}a_{2}b_{2}q^{2j};q\bigr)_{N-j}}{\bigl(t^{\alpha}a_{2}b_{2}q^{j-1};q\bigr)_{j}\bigl(t^{\alpha}a_{1}a_{2}b_{1}b_{2}q^{n+j-1};q\bigr)_{N-j}}},\qquad 0\leq j\leq N,

\displaystyle u^{A}_{ij}=\bigl(-t^{\alpha}a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}\bigr)^{j-i}q^{\left({j\atop 2}\right)-\left({i\atop 2}\right)}\begin{bmatrix}j\\
i\end{bmatrix}_{q}{\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(a_{1}b_{1}q^{N-j};q\bigr)_{j-i}}{\bigl(t^{\alpha}a_{2}b_{2}q^{2i};q\bigr)_{j-i}}},\qquad 0\leq i\leq j\leq N.

(3.15)

Remark 3.3
.

The difference equations for the shift
T_{i}=T_{t,b_{i}}^{-1}T_{t,a_{i}}
are nothing but the traditional (original)
q
-KZ equation.
The components of the
R
matrix appearing in this type of
q
-KZ equation are nothing but the connection coefficients
between two Matsuo bases
\{e_{i}(a,b)\}
with different parameters
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
.
Due to the specialization given in equation (
3.9
),
the computation can be reduced to the connection problem of single variable polynomials as in
[
50
]
.
On the other hand, the matrix
A
for the shift of
T_{\alpha}\colon\alpha\to\alpha+1

is obtained in a different manner and the basis
E_{k,i}
was introduced for this purpose in
[
29
]
.
It is the
q
-KZ equation for
T_{\alpha}
that is related to the Shakirov’s equation.

The following relation between the matrices
R
and
A
seems to be noticed long ago at least among specialists
(e.g.,
[
30
]
).
We give a proof since the relation plays an important role in Section
3.3
.

Proposition 3.4
.

We have

A=sT(R)D,

(3.16)

where
s
is a scalar and
D
is a diagonal matrix given by

s=q^{N(N-1)/2}(a_{1}a_{2}b_{2})^{N}{\displaystyle\frac{(t^{\alpha};q)_{N}}{\bigl(t^{\alpha}q^{N-1}a_{1}a_{2}b_{1}b_{2};q\bigr)_{N}}},\qquad D=\bigl((a_{1}b_{2})^{-i}\delta_{ij}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq N}.

(3.17)

T
is a shift operator acting only on the parameters
a_{2}
,
b_{2}
as

T=\bigl\{a_{2}\to a_{2}\bigl(t^{\alpha}q^{N-1}a_{1}b_{2}\bigr),\,b_{2}\to b_{2}\bigl(t^{\alpha}q^{N-1}a_{1}b_{2}\bigr)^{-1}\bigr\}.

(3.18)

Proof.

Using the relation
\bigl(x^{-1};q\bigr)_{k}=(-1)^{k}q^{k(k-1)/2}x^{-k}\bigl(xq^{1-k};q\bigr)_{k}

and the explicit forms (
3.12
) and (
3.14
), one can check

L_{A}=T(L_{R}),\qquad D_{A}=sT(D_{R})D,\qquad U_{A}=D^{-1}T(U_{R})D.

(3.19)

Then we obtain

A=L_{A}D_{A}U_{A}=T(L_{R})sT(D_{R})DD^{-1}T(U_{R})D=sT(L_{R}D_{R}U_{R})D=sT(R)D,

(3.20)

as desired.
∎

The compatibility of the dual pair of difference equations in Theorem
3.2
implies
RD_{2}A=ARD_{2}
.
In Appendix
D
, we give a direct check of the compatibility based on the matrix inversion formula of Andrews and Bressoud.
It is amusing that the compatibility condition follows from Bailey’s transformation formula for terminating very-well-poised balanced
series
{}_{10}W_{9}
.

3.2
Lattice truncation by a choice of the cycle

The sum in the Jackson integral (
3.1
) considered in
[
29
]
is bilateral, namely it is taken over

\{\nu_{i}\}\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
. On the other hand, the Nekrasov partition function involves a sum over Young diagrams.
For the function
\Psi
, the discrepancy is remedied by an appropriate choice of the cycle
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{N})
.
The suitable cycle is already known (see
[
32
, equation (3.19)]
and
[
31
, equations (4.4) and (4.5)]
).
In fact, we have the following.

Proposition 3.5
.

For
\xi
given by

\xi=\xi_{m,n}=\bigl(\underbrace{a_{2},a_{2}q,\dots,a_{2}q^{n-1}}_{n},\underbrace{a_{1},a_{1}q,\dots,a_{1}q^{m-1}}_{m}\bigr),\qquad m+n=N,

(3.21)

the lattice summation over
\bigl\{z_{i}=\xi_{i}t^{\nu_{i}}\bigr\}
is truncated to a cone

\displaystyle 0\leq\nu_{1}\leq\nu_{2}\leq\dots\leq\nu_{n-1}\leq\nu_{n},

\displaystyle 0\leq\nu_{n+1}\leq\nu_{n+2}\leq\dots\leq\nu_{N-1}\leq\nu_{N},

(3.22)

and we can normalize the
\mathbb{C}^{N+1}
-valued function (
3.7
) as

\Psi^{T}=\begin{bmatrix}\Psi_{m}\\
\vdots\\
\Psi_{0}\\
\vdots\\
\Psi_{-n}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}*&*&*&*&\cdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\cdots\\
1&*&*&*&\cdots\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\cdots\\
O&&*&*&\cdots\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\Lambda\\
\Lambda^{2}\\
\Lambda^{3}\\
\vdots\end{bmatrix},

(3.23)

where
\Lambda=t^{\alpha}
.

When we impose the mass truncation condition
d_{2}=q^{-m}
,
d_{3}=q^{-n}
the partition function becomes a Laurent
polynomial in
x
. With the normalization (
3.23
) the component
\Psi_{i}
,
{-n\leq i\leq m}
,
is to be identified with the coefficient of
x^{i}
-term in the Laurent polynomial (see also Figure
1

for the structure of the partition function).

Proof.

The condition (
3.22
) easily follows from the explicit form (
3.3
) of the function
\Phi(z)
.
We will show (
3.23
). From the expression (
3.8
) for
e_{k}=e_{k}(a_{2},b_{1},z)
with

z=\bigl(\underbrace{a_{2}t^{\nu_{1}},a_{2}qt^{\nu_{2}},\dots,a_{2}q^{n-1}t^{\nu_{n}}}_{n},\underbrace{a_{1}t^{\nu_{n+1}},a_{1}qt^{\nu_{n+2}},\dots,a_{1}q^{m-1}t^{\nu_{n+m}}}_{m}\bigr),

we see that the leading term in
\Lambda
-expansion of each component of
\Psi
has a single contribution
arising from a specific
\{\nu_{i}\}
and
J\subset[N]
,
|J|=k
.
Explicitly, for
0\leq k\leq m
, we have

e_{k}=g_{k}+O\bigl(\Lambda^{1}\bigr),\qquad g_{k}=\frac{\bigl(q^{-1};q^{-1}\bigr)_{m}(b_{1}a_{2};q)_{n}\bigl(q^{-N+k};q\bigr)_{n}\bigl(q^{k}b_{1}a_{1};q\bigr)_{m-k}\bigl(q^{-n}\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}},q\bigr)_{k}}{\bigl(1-q^{-1}\bigr)^{N}},

arising from
J=\{n+1,n+2,\dots,n+k\}
,
(\nu_{i})=\bigl(0^{N}\bigr)
,
and for
0\leq l\leq n
, we have

\displaystyle e_{m+l}=h_{l}\Lambda^{l}+O\bigl(\Lambda^{l+1}\bigr),

\displaystyle h_{l}=\frac{\bigl(q^{-1};q^{-1}\bigr)_{n-l}\bigl(q^{-1};q^{-1}\bigr)_{m+l}(t;q)_{l}(b_{1}a_{2};q)_{n-l}\bigl(q^{l-n}\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}};q\bigr)_{m}}{\bigl(1-q^{-1}\bigr)^{N}},

arising from
J=\{n-l+1,n-l+2,\dots,N\}
,
(\nu_{i})=\bigl(0^{l},1^{n-l},0^{m}\bigr)
.
Dividing by the scalar factor

h_{0}=\frac{\bigl(q^{-1};q^{-1}\bigr)_{n}\bigl(q^{-1};q^{-1}\bigr)_{m}(b_{1}a_{2};q)_{n}\bigl(q^{-n}\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}};q\bigr)_{m}}{\bigl(1-q^{-1}\bigr)^{N}},

we have the expression (
3.23
).
∎

For the function
\Psi
normalized as above, the two types of
q
-KZ equations in Theorem
3.2
can be written as

\bigl(T^{-1}_{t,a_{2}}T_{t,b_{2}}\Psi\bigr){\tilde{D}}_{1}R=\Psi,\qquad T_{t,\Lambda}\Psi=\Psi{\tilde{A}}{\tilde{D}}_{2},

(3.24)

where the matrices
{\tilde{A}}
and
R
are given as the following connection matrices
16
16

16

Contrary to the matrix
R
,
the fact that the matrix
{\tilde{A}}
is obtained as the connection matrix of Matsuo base is not obvious from the definition.

\displaystyle[e_{0}(a_{2},b_{1}),\dots,e_{N}(a_{2},b_{1})]=[e_{N}(a_{1},b_{2}),\dots,e_{0}(a_{1},b_{2})]R,

\displaystyle[e_{N}(a,b),\dots,e_{0}(a,b)]_{\begin{subarray}{c}a=a_{1}b_{1}\Lambda\\
b=q^{N-1}a_{2}b_{2}\end{subarray}}=[e_{0}(c,d),\dots,e_{N}(c,d)]_{\begin{subarray}{c}c=q^{1-N}\\
d=\Lambda^{-1}\end{subarray}}{\tilde{A}},

and
{\tilde{D}}_{1}
,

are diagonal matrices given by

\displaystyle{\tilde{D}}_{1}=(-1)^{N}q^{\frac{(N-1)(n-m)}{2}}\biggl(\frac{a_{2}}{a_{1}}\biggr)^{N}\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\frac{1-q^{m-n+1+i}\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}}{1-q^{i}a_{2}b_{1}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{{\tilde{D}}_1=}{}\times\prod_{i=0}^{m-1}\frac{1-q^{i}a_{1}b_{2}}{1-q^{n-m+1+i}\frac{a_{2}}{a_{1}}}\cdot{\rm diag}\bigl(\bigl\{\bigl(\Lambda q^{N-1}\bigr)^{m-i}\bigr\}_{i=0}^{N}\bigr),

\displaystyle{\tilde{D}}_{2}=\biggl(\frac{q^{m}a_{1}}{a_{2}}\biggr)^{n}\prod_{i=0}^{N-1}\frac{1-q^{i}\Lambda}{1-q^{N-1+i}a_{1}a_{2}b_{1}b_{2}\Lambda}\cdot{\rm diag}\bigl(\bigl\{\bigl(a_{2}b_{1}\bigr)^{N-i}\bigr\}_{i=0}^{N}\bigr).

3.3
Base-fibre duality of the
\boldsymbol{q}
-KZ equation

Let us look at the two types of the
q
-KZ equation explicitly in the case at our hand.
It turns out that these
q
-KZ equations are related by the duality which exchanges
the instanton expansion parameter
\Lambda
and the Coulomb parameter
Q
.
The
q
-KZ equation arising from Shakirov’s equation was given by

\psi_{j}(\Lambda)=\sum_{i=-n}^{m}\psi_{i}\biggl(\frac{\Lambda}{t}\biggr)\bigl(Q^{\vee}\bigr)^{i}\ r_{i,j}(\Lambda),\qquad j=-n,\dots,m,

(3.25)

where
Q^{\vee}:=(qtQ)^{-1}
is the shift parameter for
x
and

[r_{i,j}(\Lambda)]_{i,j=-n}^{m}

is the
R
-matrix obtained by the specialization
d_{2}=q^{-m}
,
d_{3}=q^{-n}
.
Since the leading term
r_{i,j}(0)
of the expansion
r_{i,j}(\Lambda)=r_{i,j}(0)+O(\Lambda)
is upper triangular
with
r_{i,i}(0)=q^{i(i+1)}
,
the equation (
3.25
) has a set of fundamental solutions of the form

\psi^{(i)}_{j}(\Lambda)=\Lambda^{\rho_{i}}Y_{i,j}(\Lambda),\qquad Y_{i,j}(\Lambda)=Y_{i,j}(0)+O(\Lambda),\qquad i,j=-n,\dots,m,

(3.26)

where
t^{\rho_{i}}=\bigl(Q^{\vee}\bigr)^{i}r_{i,i}(0)=\bigl(Q^{\vee}\bigr)^{i}q^{i(i+1)}
and the leading coefficients
Y_{i,j}(0)
are upper triangular.
One can normalize them as
Y_{i,i}(0)=1
.
Then the equation for
Y_{i,j}(\Lambda)
is written as

Y_{k,j}(\Lambda)=\sum_{i=-n}^{m}\bigl(Q^{\vee}\bigr)^{-k}q^{-k(k+1)}Y_{k,i}\biggl(\frac{\Lambda}{t}\biggr)\bigl(Q^{\vee}\bigr)^{i}r_{i,j}(\Lambda),\qquad k,j=-n,\dots,m.

(3.27)

Since
r_{i,j}(\Lambda)
is independent of
Q^{\vee}
, this equation depends on the parameter
Q^{\vee}
only through simple power factors.
The following proposition
17
17

17

Proposition
3.6
is a consequence of Theorems
3.2
and
4.1
.
The proof is omitted, since it is not used in other parts of the paper.
shows that the fundamental solution
Y(\Lambda)=Y\bigl(\Lambda,Q^{\vee}\bigr)
satisfies another
q
-KZ equation
with respect to the parameter
Q^{\vee}
.

Proposition 3.6
.

The fundamental solution
Y\bigl(\Lambda,Q^{\vee}\bigr)
satisfies the following equation:

\sum_{j=-n}^{m}Y_{i,j}\biggl(\Lambda,\frac{Q^{\vee}}{t}\biggr)\biggl(\frac{\Lambda d_{1}}{q^{m+2}}\biggr)^{j}\tilde{r}_{j,k}\bigl(Q^{\vee}\bigr)=v_{i}\bigl(\Lambda,Q^{\vee}\bigr)Y_{i,k}\bigl(\Lambda,Q^{\vee}\bigr),\qquad-n\leq i,k\leq m,

(3.28)

where

\tilde{r}_{i,j}\bigl(Q^{\vee}\bigr)=r_{i,j}(\Lambda){|}_{\Lambda\mapsto\frac{q^{m+2}}{d_{1}}Q^{\vee}}\quad

(3.29)

and

v_{i}\bigl(\Lambda,Q^{\vee}\bigr)=q^{i(i+1)}\biggl(\frac{\Lambda d_{1}}{q^{m+2}}\biggr)^{i}\frac{\bigl(Q^{\vee}q^{2+2i};q\bigr)_{m-i}\bigl(d_{4}Q^{\vee}q^{1-n};q\bigr)_{n+i}}{\bigl(d_{1}^{-1}Q^{\vee}q^{2+i};q\bigr)_{m-i}\bigl(Q^{\vee}q^{1-n+i};q\bigr)_{n+i}}.

(3.30)

The equations (
3.27
) and (
3.28
) look very similar.
To make this similarity more explicit we make the gauge transformation

Y_{i,j}\bigl(\Lambda,Q^{\vee}\bigr)=G_{i}\bigl(Q^{\vee}\bigr)\tilde{Y}_{i,j}\bigl(\Lambda,Q^{\vee}\bigr)
with

G_{i}\bigl(Q^{\vee}\bigr)=\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\bigl(Q^{\vee}q^{2+2i}t^{k};q\bigr)_{m-i}\bigl(d_{4}Q^{\vee}q^{1-n}t^{k};q\bigr)_{n+i}}{\bigl(d_{1}^{-1}Q^{\vee}q^{2+i}t^{k};q\bigr)_{m-i}\bigl(Q^{\vee}q^{1-n+i}t^{k};q\bigr)_{n+i}}.

(3.31)

Since

G_{i}^{-1}\biggl(\frac{Q^{\vee}}{t}\biggr)G_{i}\bigl(Q^{\vee}\bigr)v_{i}\bigl(\Lambda,Q^{\vee}\bigr)=q^{i(i+1)}\biggl(\frac{\Lambda d_{1}}{q^{m+2}}\biggr)^{i},

one can rewrite the equation (
3.28
) for
{-n\leq i,k\leq m}
as

\displaystyle\sum_{j=-n}^{m}{\tilde{Y}}_{i,j}\biggl(\Lambda,\frac{Q^{\vee}}{t}\biggr)\biggl(\frac{\Lambda d_{1}}{q^{m+2}}\biggr)^{j}\tilde{r}_{j,k}\bigl(Q^{\vee}\bigr)=q^{i(i+1)}\biggl(\frac{\Lambda d_{1}}{q^{m+2}}\biggr)^{i}{\tilde{Y}}_{i,k}\bigl(\Lambda,Q^{\vee}\bigr).

(3.32)

This equation is exactly the same form as (
3.27
) under the replacement

\Lambda\mapsto\frac{q^{m+2}}{d_{1}}Q^{\vee},\qquad Q^{\vee}\mapsto\frac{d_{1}}{q^{m+2}}\Lambda.

(3.33)

Figure 2:
Geometric engineering of
{\rm U}(2)
gauge theory by local
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

\Lambda
is the Kähler parameter of the base
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
Q^{\vee}
is that of the fibre.
We can introduce four matter hypermultiplets by blow-ups.

Remark 3.7
.

For the five-dimensional quantum Seiberg–Witten curve
with coefficient matrix
{\mathcal{D}}
or
{\mathcal{D}}_{3}
in the proof of Proposition 5.1 in
[
10
]
,
the positions of the external lines (tentacles of the corresponding amoeba) are given by

(x,p)=(0,d_{3}),(0,d_{4}),\biggl(\infty,\frac{1}{d_{1}}\biggr),\biggl(\infty,\frac{1}{d_{2}}\biggr),(1,0),(d_{3}d_{4}\Lambda\mu,0),\biggl(\frac{\mu q}{d_{1}d_{2}},\infty\biggr),\biggl(\frac{\Lambda}{q},\infty\biggr)

or

(x,p)=(0,d_{3}),\biggl(0,\frac{1}{\mu d_{4}}\biggr),\biggl(\infty,\frac{1}{d_{1}}\biggr),\biggl(\infty,\frac{d_{2}}{q^{2}\mu}\biggr),\biggl(\frac{q}{d_{2}},0\biggr),(d_{3}\Lambda,0),\biggl(\frac{1}{d_{1}},\infty\biggr),\biggl(\frac{d_{4}\Lambda}{q},\infty\biggr),

where
\mu=Q^{\vee}
.
Then, the exchange
\Lambda\leftrightarrow Q^{\vee}
can be seen as the exchange of two external lines at
p=\infty
(for
{\mathcal{D}}
)
or as the exchange of
x
and
p
(for
{\mathcal{D}_{3}}
) respectively.

Example 3.8
.

For
d_{2}=q^{-m}
,
d_{3}=q^{-n}
with
(m,n)=(1,0)
, the solution
Z=Z(\Lambda,x)
of Shakirov’s equation

\displaystyle T_{t,\Lambda}Z(\Lambda,x)={\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}T_{qtQ,x}^{-1}Z(\Lambda,x)

(3.34)

is explicitly given by the Heine’s series
{}_{2}\phi_{1}\bigl[{a,b\atop c};t,z\bigr]
with base
t
as

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle={}_{2}\phi_{1}\left[{\frac{1}{d_{1}},\frac{Qt}{d_{4}}\atop\frac{Qt}{q}};t,\frac{d_{1}d_{4}\Lambda}{q^{2}}\right]-\frac{1-d_{1}}{1-\frac{q}{Qt}}\ {}_{2}\phi_{1}\left[{\frac{t}{d_{1}},\frac{Qt}{d_{4}}\atop\frac{Qt^{2}}{q}};t,\frac{d_{1}d_{4}\Lambda}{q^{2}}\right]x

\displaystyle={}_{2}\phi_{1}\left[{a,z_{2}\atop bz_{2}};t,z_{1}\right]+\frac{bz_{2}\bigl(1-\frac{1}{a}\bigr)}{1-bz_{2}}\ {}_{2}\phi_{1}\left[{ta,z_{2}\atop tbz_{2}};t,z_{1}\right]x,

(3.35)

where we put
\Lambda=\frac{aqz_{1}}{b}
,

Q=\frac{bqz_{2}}{t}
,

d_{1}=\frac{1}{a}
,

d_{4}=bq

for simplicity.
Then the coefficients
y_{0}
,
y_{1}
of
Z=y_{0}+y_{1}x
satisfy

\displaystyle\biggl(1-\frac{az_{1}}{b}\biggr)T_{t,z_{1}}Y=YM(z_{1}),

(3.36)

\displaystyle\biggl(1-\frac{t}{bz_{2}}\biggr)T_{t,z_{2}}^{-1}Y=YM\biggl(\frac{t}{z_{2}}\biggr),

(3.37)

where

Y=[y_{0},y_{1}],\qquad M(u)=\begin{bmatrix}1&\\
&\frac{az_{1}}{bz_{2}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1-\frac{u}{b}&1-\frac{1}{a}\\[4.2679pt]
1-\frac{1}{b}&1-\frac{1}{au}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&\\
&-1\end{bmatrix}.

(3.38)

The equation (
3.36
) is the truncated form of (
3.34
), while
the equation (
3.37
) follows from

\displaystyle b^{-1}\biggl(1-\frac{t}{z_{2}}\biggr)T_{t,z_{2}}^{-1}{\tilde{Y}}={\tilde{Y}}M\biggl(\frac{t}{z_{2}}\biggr),

(3.39)

where
{\tilde{Y}}=[{\tilde{y}}_{0},{\tilde{y}}_{1}]
,
{\tilde{Z}}={\tilde{y}}_{0},+{\tilde{y}}_{1}x
,
and

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}:={}_{2}\phi_{1}\biggl[{b,z_{1}\atop az_{1}};t,z_{2}\biggr]+\frac{bz_{2}(1-\frac{1}{a})}{1-az_{1}}{}_{2}\phi_{1}\biggl[{tb,z_{1}\atop taz_{1}};t,z_{2}\biggr]x{\displaystyle\frac{(bz_{2},z_{1};t)_{\infty}}{(az_{1},z_{2};t)_{\infty}}}Z.

(3.40)

For
x=0
, the last relation in (
3.40
) is Heine’s transformation. Note that the ratio
{\tilde{Z}}/Z
is independent of
x
,
which explains how a single function can satisfy the dual pair of equations (
3.36
) and (
3.37
).

4
Nekrasov partition function as Jackson integral

Let us show that the
K
-theoretic Nekrasov partition function from the affine Laumon space agrees with the Matsuo bases.
When
n=2
the general formula (
F.28
) derived in Appendix
F
for the orbifolded Nekrasov factor
reduces to

\displaystyle\mathsf{N}_{\lambda,\mu}^{(0|2)}(u|q,t)=\prod_{i,j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\Bigl[uq^{j-i}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{\mu_{i}^{\vee}-\lambda_{j}^{\vee}}{2}\rfloor};t\Bigr]_{\infty}}{\Bigl[uq^{j-i-1}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{\mu_{i}^{\vee}-\lambda_{j}^{\vee}}{2}\rfloor};t\Bigr]_{\infty}}\frac{\bigl[uq^{j-i-1}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[uq^{j-i}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}},

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{N}_{\lambda,\mu}^{(1|2)}(u|q,t)=\prod_{i,j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\Bigl[uq^{j-i}t^{\frac{1}{2}+\lfloor\frac{\mu_{i}^{\vee}-\lambda_{j}^{\vee}+1}{2}\rfloor};t\Bigr]_{\infty}}{\Bigl[uq^{j-i-1}t^{\frac{1}{2}+\lfloor\frac{\mu_{i}^{\vee}-\lambda_{j}^{\vee}+1}{2}\rfloor};t\Bigr]_{\infty}}\frac{\bigl[uq^{j-i-1}t^{\frac{1}{2}};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[uq^{j-i}t^{\frac{1}{2}};t\bigr]_{\infty}},

(4.2)

where
\mu_{i}^{\vee}
and
\lambda_{j}^{\vee}
denote the transpose of the Young diagrams.
For the definition of
[u;t]_{\infty}
, we refer to (
F.5
).
When one of the partitions is empty, the formula simplifies to

\displaystyle\mathsf{N}_{\lambda,\varnothing}^{(0|2)}(u|q,\kappa)=\prod_{i\geq 1}\bigl[uq^{i-1};\kappa^{2}\bigr]_{\lfloor\frac{\lambda_{i}^{\vee}+1}{2}\rfloor},\qquad\mathsf{N}_{\lambda,\varnothing}^{(1|2)}(u|q,\kappa)=\prod_{i\geq 1}\bigl[uq^{i-1}\kappa;\kappa^{2}\bigr]_{\lfloor\frac{\lambda_{i}^{\vee}}{2}\rfloor},

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathsf{N}_{\varnothing,\mu}^{(0|2)}(u|q,\kappa)=\prod_{i\geq 1}\Bigl[uq^{-i}\kappa^{-2\lfloor\frac{\mu_{i}^{\vee}}{2}\rfloor};\kappa^{2}\Bigr]_{\lfloor\frac{\mu_{i}^{\vee}}{2}\rfloor},

\displaystyle\mathsf{N}_{\varnothing,\mu}^{(1|2)}(u|q,\kappa)=\prod_{i\geq 1}\Bigl[uq^{-i}\kappa^{1-2\lfloor\frac{\mu_{i}^{\vee}+1}{2}\rfloor};\kappa^{2}\Bigr]_{\lfloor\frac{\mu_{i}^{\vee}+1}{2}\rfloor},

(4.4)

where
t=\kappa^{-2}
.
Note that
\lfloor\frac{m+1}{2}\rfloor+\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor=m
. When
\lambda_{i}^{\vee}
,
\mu_{i}^{\vee}
are even,

\mathsf{N}^{(0|2)}
and
\mathsf{N}^{(1|2)}
have the same number of factors.
But when they are odd,
\lambda_{i}^{\vee}
contributes more to
\mathsf{N}^{(0|2)}
, while
\mu_{i}^{\vee}
does to

\mathsf{N}^{(1|2)}
. This is due to a difference of the coloring of
\lambda
and
\mu
(see Figure
3
).

\bigl(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)}\bigr)=\biggl(~~{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}*(orange)\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower
