height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 345.4pt}\kern 690.4pt}\kern 690.4pt}\kern 690.4pt}\kern 690.4pt}}~~\biggr),\qquad\bigl(\lambda^{(1)}\bigr)^{\vee}=(4,2,1,1),\qquad\bigl(\lambda^{(2)}\bigr)^{\vee}=(3,2).

Figure 3:
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-coloring of a pair of Young diagrams.

Recall that by the localization formula the Nekrasov partition function with a surface defect is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{AL}}

\displaystyle{}=\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{AL}}\left(\left.\left.\begin{array}[]{c}u_{1},u_{2}\\
v_{1},v_{2}\\
w_{1},w_{2}\end{array}\right|x_{1},x_{2}\right|q,t\right)

\displaystyle{}=\sum_{\bigl(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)}\bigr)}\prod_{i,j=1}^{2}\frac{{\mathsf{N}}^{(j-i|2)}_{\varnothing,\lambda^{(j)}}(u_{i}/v_{j}|q,t){\mathsf{N}}^{(j-i|2)}_{\lambda^{(i)},\varnothing}(v_{i}/w_{j}|q,t)}{{\mathsf{N}}^{(j-i|2)}_{\lambda^{(i)},\lambda^{(j)}}(v_{i}/v_{j}|q,t)}\cdot x_{1}^{|\lambda^{(1)}|_{o}+|\lambda^{(2)}|_{e}}x_{2}^{|\lambda^{(1)}|_{e}+|\lambda^{(2)}|_{o}},

where
\bigl(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)}\bigr)
is a fixed point of the toric action on the affine Laumon space.
The weight in the summation over the fixed points is

x_{1}^{|\lambda^{(1)}|_{o}+|\lambda^{(2)}|_{e}}x_{2}^{|\lambda^{(1)}|_{e}+|\lambda^{(2)}|_{o}}
,
where
|\lambda|_{o}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{2k-1}
and
|\lambda|_{e}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{2k}
.
The expansion parameters
(x_{1},x_{2})
are related to the physical parameters
(\Lambda,x)
by

\displaystyle x_{1}=-\frac{\sqrt{Qd_{1}d_{2}}}{\kappa}x,\qquad x_{2}=-\sqrt{\frac{d_{3}d_{4}}{q^{2}Q}}\frac{\Lambda}{x}.

(4.8)

By the relations (
2.2
) and (
2.3
), this specialization gives

x_{1}=-t^{1/2}T_{1}^{1/2}T_{2}^{1/2}x
and
x_{2}=-t^{1/2}T_{3}^{1/2}T_{4}^{1/2}\frac{\Lambda}{x}
.
We also employ the following specialization of the spectral parameters
with
\kappa=t^{-\frac{1}{2}}
:

u_{1}=\frac{qQ}{d_{3}},\quad u_{2}=\frac{\kappa q}{d_{1}},\qquad v_{1}=1,\quad v_{2}=\frac{Q}{\kappa},\qquad w_{1}=\frac{1}{d_{2}},\quad w_{2}=\frac{Q}{d_{4}\kappa}.

(4.9)

One can check that these specializations agree those of
\mathcal{F}^{(1)}
defined in
[
10
, Section 6]
.
The overall scaling of parameters by
Q^{1/2}
is necessary for the matching of the parameters
v_{i}

of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{AL}}
and
\mathcal{F}^{(1)}
.
After the same scaling the specialization for the function
\mathcal{F}^{(1)}
is

u_{1}=q^{1/2}\kappa^{-1}QT_{3},\quad u_{2}=q^{1/2}T_{1}^{-1},\qquad w_{1}=q^{-1/2}\kappa^{-1}QT_{2},\quad w_{2}=q^{-1/2}T_{4}^{-1}.

(4.10)

By substituting (
2.3
), we can see the agreement with (
4.9
).

There are four possibilities of a specialization of the spectral parameters
u_{i}
and
w_{i}

due to the symmetry
d_{1}\leftrightarrow d_{2}
,
d_{3}\leftrightarrow d_{4}
of the Hamiltonian
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}
.
But the symmetry is broken by choosing the specialization (
4.9
).
The
are paired into two
{\rm SU}(2)
doublets;
(d_{1},d_{3})
and
(d_{2},d_{4})

in the case of the affine Laumon space.
Such a rearrangement of
{\rm SU}(2)
doublets of mass parameters between the five point conformal block with a degenerate field insertion and
the four point current block was already observed in the four-dimensional theory (see
[
9
, equation (5.33)]
).
From the viewpoint of the orbifold coloring of four mass parameters, the decomposition into the pairs
(d_{1},d_{2})
and
(d_{3},d_{4})

is by the parity of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
coloring. On the other hand, the decomposition into
(d_{1},d_{3})
and
(d_{2},d_{4})
corresponds to
the fundamental and the anti-fundamental representations of
{\rm SU}(2)
gauge symmetry.

Let us introduce a notation for the length of the columns of the Young diagrams,

\displaystyle\bigl(\lambda^{(1)}\bigr)^{\vee}=(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\dots),\qquad\bigl(\lambda^{(2)}\bigr)^{\vee}=(k_{1},k_{2},\dots).

(4.11)

Omitting the normalization factors, which are the last factors in (
4.1
) and (
4.2
),
we find the following contributions to the partition function (
4
):

1.

Fundamental and anti-fundamental matter contribution

\displaystyle\prod_{i,j=1}^{2}{\mathsf{N}}^{(j-i|2)}_{\varnothing,\lambda^{(j)}}(u_{i}/v_{j}|q,t){\mathsf{N}}^{(j-i|2)}_{\lambda^{(i)},\varnothing}(v_{i}/w_{j}|q,t)\propto\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\bigl[d_{2}q^{i-1}t^{1-\lfloor\frac{\ell_{i}+1}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[d_{4}^{-1}Qq^{1-i}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\times\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\bigl[d_{3}q^{i-1}t^{-\lfloor\frac{k_{i}-1}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[Q^{-1}d_{3}q^{i-1}t^{-\lfloor\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[Qd_{2}q^{i-1}t^{1-\lfloor\frac{k_{i}}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[d_{1}^{-1}Q^{-1}q^{1-i}t^{\lfloor\frac{k_{i}}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[d_{1}^{-1}q^{1-i}t^{\lfloor\frac{\ell_{i}+1}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[d_{4}^{-1}q^{1-i}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{k_{i}-1}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}.

(4.12)

2.

Vector multiplet contribution

\displaystyle\Biggl(\prod_{i,j=1}^{2}{\mathsf{N}}^{(j-i|2)}_{\lambda^{(i)},\lambda^{(j)}}(v_{i}/v_{j}|q,t)\Biggr)^{-1}\!\!\!\propto\!\prod_{i,j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\bigl[q^{j-i-1}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{\ell_{i}-\ell_{j}}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[q^{j-i}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{\ell_{i}-\ell_{j}}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\!\prod_{i,j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\bigl[q^{j-i-1}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{k_{i}-k_{j}}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[q^{j-i}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{k_{i}-k_{j}}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\times\prod_{i,j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\bigl[Q^{-1}q^{j-i-1}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{k_{i}-\ell_{j}-1}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[Q^{-1}q^{j-i}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{k_{i}-\ell_{j}-1}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\prod_{i,j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\bigl[Qq^{j-i-1}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{\ell_{i}-k_{j}+1}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[Qq^{j-i}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{\ell_{i}-k_{j}+1}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}.

(4.13)

We note that for a given pair of Young diagrams
\bigl(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)}\bigr)
, the normalization factor reduces
the formal infinite products of ratio of
[u;t]_{\infty}
in the above formulas to finite products due to the cancellations
between the numerator and the denominator for sufficiently large
i
and
j
. To see it,
note that we have
\ell_{i}=0
for
\mathsf{m}:=\bigl(\lambda^{(1)}\bigr)_{1}<i
and
k_{j}=0
for
\mathsf{n}:=\bigl(\lambda^{(2)}\bigr)_{1}<j
.
Later we will make a tuning of mass parameters so that we have
\mathsf{m}=m
and
\mathsf{n}=n
uniformly for all
\bigl(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)}\bigr)

that contribute to the partition function.
In terms of the variables
18
18

18

Note that

\lfloor\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\rfloor
and
\lfloor\frac{k_{j}}{2}\rfloor
count the vertical dominos of length 2
in the Young diagrams
\lambda
and
\mu
, respectively.

z_{i}=q^{1-i}t^{\lfloor\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\rfloor},\qquad w_{j}=q^{1-j}t^{\lfloor\frac{k_{j}}{2}\rfloor},

(4.14)

the finite product form of the matter contribution is

\prod_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}}\frac{\bigl[d_{2}z_{i}^{-1}t^{1-(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[Q^{-1}d_{3}z_{i}^{-1};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[d_{4}^{-1}Qz_{i}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[d_{1}^{-1}z_{i}t^{(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\prod_{j=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\frac{\bigl[d_{3}w_{j}^{-1}t^{1-(k_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[Qd_{2}w_{j}^{-1}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[d_{1}^{-1}Q^{-1}w_{j};t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[d_{4}^{-1}w_{j}t^{(k_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}},

(4.15)

where we have used the identity

\biggl\lfloor\frac{K-L}{2}\biggr\rfloor=\biggl\lfloor\frac{K}{2}\biggr\rfloor-\biggl\lfloor\frac{L}{2}\biggr\rfloor+\{(K)-1\}\cdot(L),\qquad K,L\in\mathbb{Z}

(4.16)

by the specialization
L=\pm 1
. Here
(K)
denotes the parity of an integer
K
;
(K)=1
for an odd integer and
(K)=0
, otherwise.

For the vector multiplet contribution, we have to make
an appropriate decomposition of
(i,j)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
into four regions
R_{\mathrm{I}},\dots,R_{\mathrm{IV}}

according to the lengths of the first row of the Young diagrams;
\mathsf{m}=\bigl(\lambda^{(1)}\bigr)_{1}
and
\mathsf{n}=\bigl(\lambda^{(2)}\bigr)_{1}
.
For example, for a pair
(\ell_{i},k_{j})
, we define

\displaystyle R_{\mathrm{I}}=\{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{m},\,1\leq j\leq\mathsf{n}\},

\displaystyle\qquad R_{\mathrm{II}}=\{\mathsf{m}+1\leq i<\infty,\,1\leq j\leq\mathsf{n}\},

\displaystyle R_{\mathrm{III}}=\{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{m},\,\mathsf{n}+1\leq j<\infty\},

\displaystyle\qquad R_{\mathrm{IV}}=\{\mathsf{m}+1\leq i<\infty,\,\mathsf{n}+1\leq j<\infty\},\hskip-28.45274pt

(4.17)

and for a pair
(\ell_{i},\ell_{j})
we set
\mathsf{m}=\mathsf{n}
in the decomposition (
4.17
).
The vector multiplet contribution becomes trivial only for
R_{\mathrm{IV}}
.
We find the vector multiplet contributions are

(I)

From the region
R_{\mathrm{I}}
,

\displaystyle\prod_{i\neq j=1}^{\mathsf{m}}\frac{\bigl[(tz_{i}/qz_{j})~t^{\{(\ell_{i})-1\}\cdot(\ell_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[(tz_{i}/z_{j})~t^{\{(\ell_{i})-1\}\cdot(\ell_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\prod_{i\neq j=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\frac{\bigl[(tw_{i}/qw_{j})~t^{\{(k_{i})-1\}\cdot(k_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[(tw_{i}/w_{j})~t^{\{(k_{i})-1\}\cdot(k_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\times\prod_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}}\prod_{j=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\frac{\bigl[\bigl(Q^{-1}w_{j}/qz_{i}\bigr)~t^{(k_{j})\cdot\{1-(\ell_{i})\}};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[\bigl(Q^{-1}w_{j}/z_{i}\bigr)~t^{(k_{j})\cdot\{1-(\ell_{i})\}};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\frac{\bigl[(Qtz_{i}/qw_{j})~t^{(\ell_{i})\cdot\{1-(k_{j})\}};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[(Qtz_{i}/w_{j})~t^{(\ell_{i})\cdot\{1-(k_{j})\}};t\bigr]_{\infty}}.

(4.18)

Here and henceforth, we use a shorthand notation
\prod_{i\neq j=1}^{\mathsf{m}}

for the sum over
1\leq i,j\leq\mathsf{m}
without the diagonal part
i=j
.

(II)

For two semi-infinite regions
R_{\mathrm{II}}
and
R_{\mathrm{III}}
, only the “boundary” contributions remain,

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}}\frac{\bigl[q^{\mathsf{m}-i}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[q^{i-\mathsf{m}-1}t^{1+\lfloor-\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\frac{\bigl[Qq^{\mathsf{n}-i}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{\ell_{i}+1}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[Q^{-1}q^{i-\mathsf{n}-1}t^{\lfloor\frac{1-\ell_{i}}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\times\prod_{j=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\frac{\bigl[q^{\mathsf{n}-j}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{k_{j}}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[q^{j-\mathsf{n}-1}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{-k_{j}}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\frac{\bigl[Q^{-1}q^{\mathsf{m}-j}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{k_{j}-1}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[Qq^{j-\mathsf{m}-1}t^{1+\lfloor\frac{1-k_{j}}{2}\rfloor};t\bigr]_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\prod_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}}\frac{\bigl[z_{i}q^{\mathsf{m}-1}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[z_{i}^{-1}q^{-\mathsf{m}}t^{1-(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\frac{\bigl[Qz_{i}q^{\mathsf{n}-1}t^{1+(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[Q^{-1}z_{i}^{-1}q^{-\mathsf{n}};t\bigr]_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\times\prod_{j=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\frac{\bigl[w_{j}q^{\mathsf{n}-1}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[w_{j}^{-1}q^{-\mathsf{n}}t^{1-(k_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\frac{\bigl[Q^{-1}w_{j}q^{\mathsf{m}-1}t^{(k_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[Qw_{j}^{-1}q^{-\mathsf{m}}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}},

(4.19)

where we have used (
4.16
).

In summary, up to a
z_{i}
,
w_{j}
independent normalization factor, we can rewrite the Nekrasov partition function
as a sum over the positive cone of a lattice with the following weight function:

\displaystyle W_{\mathsf{m}+\mathsf{n}}(z)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}}\frac{\bigl[d_{2}z_{i}^{-1}t^{1-(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[Q^{-1}d_{3}z_{i}^{-1};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[d_{4}^{-1}Qz_{i}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[d_{1}^{-1}z_{i}t^{(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\frac{\bigl[z_{i}q^{\mathsf{m}-1}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[z_{i}^{-1}q^{-\mathsf{m}}t^{1-(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\frac{\bigl[Qz_{i}q^{\mathsf{n}-1}t^{1+(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[Q^{-1}z_{i}^{-1}q^{-\mathsf{n}};t\bigr]_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\quad{}\times\prod_{j=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\frac{\bigl[Q^{-1}d_{3}z_{\mathsf{m}+j}^{-1};t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[d_{2}z_{\mathsf{m}+j}^{-1}t^{(k_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[d_{1}^{-1}z_{\mathsf{m}+j}t^{1-(k_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[d_{4}^{-1}Qz_{\mathsf{m}+j}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}}\frac{\bigl[Qz_{\mathsf{m}+j}q^{\mathsf{n}-1}t^{2-(k_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[Q^{-1}z_{\mathsf{m}+j}^{-1}q^{-\mathsf{n}};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\frac{\bigl[z_{\mathsf{m}+j}q^{\mathsf{m}-1}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[z_{\mathsf{m}+j}^{-1}q^{-\mathsf{m}}t^{(k_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\quad{}\times\prod_{i\neq j=1}^{\mathsf{m}}\frac{\bigl[(tz_{i}/qz_{j})~t^{\{(\ell_{i})-1\}\cdot(\ell_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[(tz_{i}/z_{j})~t^{\{(\ell_{i})-1\}\cdot(\ell_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\prod_{i\neq j=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\frac{\bigl[(tz_{\mathsf{m}+i}/qz_{\mathsf{m}+j})~t^{(k_{i})\cdot\{(k_{j})-1\}};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[(tz_{\mathsf{m}+i}/z_{\mathsf{m}+j})~t^{(k_{i})\cdot\{(k_{j})-1\}};t\bigr]_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\quad{}\times\prod_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}}\prod_{j=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\frac{\bigl[(tz_{\mathsf{m}+j}/qz_{i})~t^{-(k_{j})\cdot(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[(tz_{\mathsf{m}+j}/z_{i})~t^{-(k_{j})\cdot(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\frac{\bigl[(tz_{i}/qz_{\mathsf{m}+j})~t^{\{(\ell_{i})-1\}\cdot\{1-(k_{j})\}};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[(tz_{i}/z_{\mathsf{m}+j})~t^{\{(\ell_{i})-1\}\cdot\{1-(k_{j})\}};t\bigr]_{\infty}},

(4.20)

where we have defined
19
19

19

See (
4.16
).

z_{\mathsf{m}+j}=Q^{-1}w_{j}t^{(k_{j})-1}=Q^{-1}q^{1-j}t^{\lfloor\frac{k_{j}-1}{2}\rfloor}
.

In the above computations, we have used the fact that
that
\ell_{i}=0
for
\mathsf{m}<i
and
k_{j}=0
for
{\mathsf{n}<j}
, where
\mathsf{m}
and
\mathsf{n}
depend on
\bigl(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)}\bigr)
.
But as is shown in Appendix
E
, by tuning some of mass parameters
d_{2}=q^{-m}
and
d_{3}=q^{-n}
,
we can assume
\mathsf{m}=m
and
\mathsf{n}=n
for any pair of Young diagrams
\bigl(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)}\bigr)
, because
a pair
\bigl(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)}\bigr)
with
\mathsf{m}>m
or
\mathsf{n}>n
does not contribute to the partition function.
Furthermore, the substitution of the mass tuning condition
d_{2}=q^{-m}
and
d_{3}=q^{-n}
leads nice cancellation in
the weight function (
4.20
).
By defining
(\ell_{m+j})=1-(k_{j})
, we can make
W_{m,n}(z,w)
completely symmetric in
z
and
w
,

\displaystyle W_{m+n}(z)=\prod_{I=1}^{m+n}\frac{\bigl[z_{I}q^{m-1}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[Qz_{I}q^{n-1}t^{1+(\ell_{I})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[d_{4}^{-1}Qz_{I}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[d_{1}^{-1}z_{I}t^{(\ell_{I})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{W_{m+n}(z)=}{}\times{}\prod_{I\neq J=1}^{m+n}\frac{\bigl[(tz_{I}/qz_{J})~t^{\{(\ell_{I})-1\}\cdot(\ell_{J})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[(tz_{I}/z_{J})~t^{\{(\ell_{I})-1\}\cdot(\ell_{J})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}.

(4.21)

In the expansion of Nekrasov partition function,
the contribution from a fixed point
\bigl(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)}\bigr)
has
the weight
x_{1}^{|\lambda^{(1)}|_{o}+|\lambda^{(2)}|_{e}}x_{2}^{|\lambda^{(1)}|_{e}+|\lambda^{(2)}|_{o}}
,
where

|\lambda|_{o}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{2k-1}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\biggl\lfloor\frac{\lambda_{k}^{\vee}+1}{2}\biggr\rfloor,\qquad|\lambda|_{e}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{2k}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\biggl\lfloor\frac{\lambda_{k}^{\vee}}{2}\biggr\rfloor.

(4.22)

Recall that we have specialized
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
as (
4.8
), which implies that
the dependence of the weight on
\Lambda
and
x
is

\Lambda^{|\lambda^{(1)}|_{e}+|\lambda^{(2)}|_{o}}x^{|\lambda^{(1)}|_{o}-|\lambda^{(1)}|_{e}+|\lambda^{(2)}|_{e}-|\lambda^{(2)}|_{o}}.

(4.23)

Since
|\lambda|_{o}-|\lambda|_{e}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\bigl(\lambda_{k}^{\vee}\bigr)
,
the power of
x
, which is identified with the
{\rm SU}(2)
spin variable, is

2s=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\ell_{i})-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(k_{j})=\sum_{I=1}^{m+n}(\ell_{I})-n.

(4.24)

Hence, only columns with odd length can produce a non-vanishing power of
x
. We see that
the range of the power
p
of
x
is
-n\leq p\leq m
(see also Figure
1
).
Similarly, the power of
\Lambda
, which we identify as the instanton number, is

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\biggl\lfloor\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\biggr\rfloor+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\biggl\lfloor\frac{k_{j}+1}{2}\biggr\rfloor=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\biggl\lfloor\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\biggr\rfloor+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\biggl\lfloor\frac{k_{j}}{2}\biggr\rfloor+\sum_{j=1}^{n}(k_{j}).

(4.25)

Now we are ready to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 4.1
.

Under the identification of the parameters

\displaystyle d_{1}=\frac{1}{q^{m-1}a_{1}b_{1}},\qquad d_{2}=q^{-m},\qquad d_{3}=q^{-n},\qquad d_{4}=\frac{1}{q^{n-1}a_{2}b_{2}},

\displaystyle Q=\frac{q^{m-n}a_{1}}{ta_{2}},

(4.26)

the affine Laumon partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{\rm AL}
(
4
) coincides with
the
\mathbb{C}^{N+1}
-valued function (
3.7
) with the integration cycle
\xi
of (
3.21
).
More precisely, the
N+1
components of
\mathcal{Z}_{\rm AL}
with respect to the expansion parameter
x

are identified as the Jackson integrals
\langle e_{k}(a_{2},b_{1}),\xi\rangle
with respect to the Matsuo basis
e_{k}
,
0\leq k\leq N=m+n
.

Proof.

Rewriting the dummy indices in (
4.21
), we consider

W_{N}(z)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\bigl[z_{i}q^{m-1}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[Qz_{i}q^{n-1}t^{1+(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[d_{4}^{-1}Qtz_{i};t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[d_{1}^{-1}z_{i}t^{(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\prod_{i\neq j=1}^{N}\frac{\bigl[(tz_{i}/qz_{j})~t^{\{(\ell_{i})-1\}\cdot(\ell_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[(tz_{i}/z_{j})~t^{\{(\ell_{i})-1\}\cdot(\ell_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}},

(4.27)

where
N=m+n
. By the parameter relation (
4.26
), it can be written as

\displaystyle W_{N}(z)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\bigl[z_{i}q^{m-1}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[q^{m-1}\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}z_{i}t^{(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[q^{m-1}a_{1}b_{2}z_{i};t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[q^{m-1}a_{1}b_{1}z_{i}t^{(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{W_{N}(z)=}{}\times\prod_{i\neq j=1}^{N}\frac{\bigl[(tz_{i}/qz_{j})t^{\{(\ell_{i})-1\}\cdot(\ell_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[(tz_{i}/z_{j})t^{\{(\ell_{i})-1\}\cdot(\ell_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}.

(4.28)

Rescaling the integration variables as
z_{i}\to z_{i}/\bigl(q^{m-1}a_{1}\bigr)
, we have

W_{N}(z)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\bigl[\frac{z_{i}}{a_{1}}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[\frac{z_{i}}{a_{2}}t^{(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{[b_{2}z_{i};t]_{\infty}\bigl[b_{1}z_{i}t^{(\ell_{i})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}\prod_{i\neq j=1}^{N}\frac{\bigl[(tz_{i}/qz_{j})~t^{\{(\ell_{i})-1\}\cdot(\ell_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}{\bigl[(tz_{i}/z_{j})~t^{\{(\ell_{i})-1\}\cdot(\ell_{j})};t\bigr]_{\infty}}.

(4.29)

We decompose the index set as

I\cup J=\{1,2,\dots,N\},\qquad I=\{i|(\ell_{i})=0\},\quad J=\{j|(\ell_{j})=1\}.

(4.30)

From (
4.24
), we see that
2s+n=|J|
. Then

W_{N}(z)=W_{N}^{(0)}(z)P_{J}(z),

(4.31)

where

\displaystyle W^{(0)}_{N}(z)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\bigl[\frac{z_{i}}{a_{1}}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}\bigl[\frac{z_{i}}{a_{2}}t;t\bigr]_{\infty}}{[b_{2}z_{i};t]_{\infty}[b_{1}z_{i};t]_{\infty}}\prod_{i\neq j=1}^{N}\frac{[tz_{i}/qz_{j};t]_{\infty}}{[tz_{i}/z_{j};t]_{\infty}},

(4.32)

\displaystyle P_{J}(z)=\prod_{j\in J}\frac{1-b_{1}z_{j}}{1-\frac{z_{j}}{a_{2}}}\times\prod_{i\in I}\prod_{j\in J}\frac{z_{j}-q^{-1}z_{i}}{z_{j}-z_{i}}.

(4.33)

Putting
z_{i}=\xi_{i}t^{\nu_{i}}
,
i=1,\dots,N
,
we can rewrite the sum of
W_{N}(z)
over the partitions
\mu
and
\lambda

as the sum of
W_{N}(z)=W_{N}^{(0)}(z)P_{J}(z)
over
\nu_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N}
and
J\subset\{1,2,\dots,N\}
,
where the correspondence is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2\nu_{i},&i\in J,\\
2\nu_{i}-1,&i\notin J,\end{cases}\qquad i=1,\dots,n,

(4.34)

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2\nu_{i+n}+1,&i+n\in J,\\
2\nu_{i+n},&i+n\notin J,\end{cases}\qquad i=1,\dots,m.

(4.35)

Then the latter sum can be identified with the Jackson integral studied in Section
3
.
In fact, from (
3.3
) and (
3.6
), we see that
W^{(0)}(z)=\Delta(z,1)\Phi(z)
.
The sum of
P_{J}(z)
for
|J|=k
and the
k
-th cocycle factor
{\hat{e}}_{k}
(
3.8
) is related by

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subset\{1,2,\dots,N\}\\
|J|=k\end{subarray}}P_{J}(z)=c_{k}{\hat{e}}_{k}(z)\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{1-\frac{z_{i}}{a_{2}}},

(4.36)

where
c_{k}=q^{(k-m)(k-n)/2}\bigl[{N\atop k}\bigr]_{q^{-1}}/\bigl[{N\atop m}\bigr]_{q^{-1}}
.
∎

5
Four-dimensional limit and KZ equation

In
[
10
]
, we computed the four-dimensional limit of the Hamiltonian
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}
and
confirmed that Shakirov’s non-stationary difference equation has the correct four-dimensional limit.
In Section
2
, we have shown the representation matrix of
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}
is nothing but the
R
-matrix of

U_{\mathsf{v}}\bigl(A_{1}^{(1)}\bigr)
with generic spins. Let us work out the four-dimensional limit of the
R
-matrix.

Proposition 5.1
.

Under the limit
q=e^{h}
,
d_{i}=q^{m_{i}}
,
h\to 0
, we have

R=1+hR^{(1)}+O\bigl(h^{2}\bigr),

(5.1)

where
R^{(1)}
is a tridiagonal matrix, which is generically infinite-dimensional.
Furthermore, it can be truncated to a finite matrix of size
m+n+1
,
if
m_{1}

(\text{or}\ m_{2})=-m
and
m_{3}

(\text{or}\ m_{4})=-n
for
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

For example, in case of
m_{1}=-2
,
m_{3}=-1
, we have

R^{(1)}=\begin{bmatrix}-\frac{3(\Lambda m_{2}-\Lambda)}{\Lambda-1}&\frac{3(m_{2}-1)}{\Lambda-1}&0&0\vskip 2.84526pt\\
\frac{\Lambda m_{4}}{\Lambda-1}&-\frac{2\Lambda m_{2}+\Lambda m_{4}}{\Lambda-1}&\frac{2m_{2}}{\Lambda-1}&0\vskip 2.84526pt\\
0&\frac{2\Lambda(m_{4}-1)}{\Lambda-1}&-\frac{\Lambda+\Lambda m_{2}+2\Lambda m_{4}-2}{\Lambda-1}&-\frac{-m_{2}-1}{\Lambda-1}\vskip 2.84526pt\\
0&0&\frac{3(\Lambda m_{4}-2\Lambda)}{\Lambda-1}&-\frac{3(\Lambda m_{4}-2)}{\Lambda-1}\end{bmatrix}.

Proof.

Under the limit
q=e^{h}
,
h\to 0
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{(-q^{\alpha}x;q)_{\infty}}{(-q^{\beta}x;q)_{\infty}}

\displaystyle=(1+x)^{\beta-\alpha}\biggl\{1-\frac{h}{2}(\alpha-\beta)(\alpha+\beta-1)\frac{x}{x-1}+O\bigl(h^{2}\bigr)\biggr\}.

(5.2)

Then the defining relation of the matrix
R=(r_{i,j})

\displaystyle q^{i(i+1)/2}x^{i}\frac{\bigl(-q^{i}d_{1}d_{2}x;q\bigr)_{\infty}}{(-d_{2}x;q)_{\infty}}\frac{\bigl(-q^{-i}d_{3}d_{4}\frac{\Lambda}{x};q\bigr)_{\infty}}{\bigl(-d_{4}\frac{\Lambda}{x};q\bigr)_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{j}r_{i,j}q^{-j(j+1)/2}x^{j}\frac{(-d_{1}x;q)_{\infty}}{\bigl(-q^{-j}x;q\bigr)_{\infty}}\frac{\bigl(-d_{3}\frac{\Lambda}{x};q\bigr)_{\infty}}{\bigl(-q^{j}\frac{\Lambda}{x};q\bigr)_{\infty}},

(5.3)

can be written as

A_{i}=\sum_{j}\biggl(\frac{x+\Lambda}{x+1}\biggr)^{j-i}B_{j}r_{i,j},

(5.4)

where up to
O\bigl(h^{2}\bigr)
,

\displaystyle A_{i}=1+\frac{1}{2}h\biggl\{-\frac{\Lambda(i-m_{3})(i-m_{3}-2m_{4}+1)}{x+\Lambda}

\displaystyle\hphantom{A_i=+\frac{1}{2} h \biggl\lx@text@lbrace-}{}-\frac{x(i+m_{1})(i+m_{1}+2m_{2}-1)}{x+1}+i(i+1)\biggr\},

\displaystyle B_{j}=1+\frac{1}{2}h\biggl\{\frac{\Lambda(j-m_{3})(j+m_{3}-1)}{x+\Lambda}+\frac{x(j-m_{1}+1)(j+m_{1})}{x+1}-j(j+1)\biggr\}.

(5.5)

We put
r_{i,j}=r^{(0)}_{i,j}+hr^{(1)}_{i,j}+O\bigl(h^{2}\bigr)
.
From the leading term, we have

\sum_{j}\biggl(\frac{x+\Lambda}{x+1}\biggr)^{j-i}r^{(0)}_{i,j}=1,

(5.6)

hence

r^{(0)}_{i,j}=\delta_{i,j}.

(5.7)

Then, from
O(h)
terms, we obtain

\sum_{j}\biggl(\frac{x+\Lambda}{x+1}\biggr)^{j-i}r^{(1)}_{i,j}=i(i+1)+\frac{x(i+m_{1})(i+m_{2})}{1+x}+\frac{\Lambda(i-m_{3})(i-m_{4})}{x+\Lambda},

(5.8)

and the solution is given by

\displaystyle r^{(1)}_{i,i-1}=\frac{\Lambda(i-m_{3})(i-m_{4})}{\Lambda-1},

\displaystyle r^{(1)}_{i,i}=-\frac{\Lambda\{(i+m_{1})(i+m_{2})+(i-m_{3})(i-m_{4})\}}{\Lambda-1}+i(i+1),

\displaystyle r^{(1)}_{i,i+1}=\frac{(i+m_{1})(i+m_{2})}{\Lambda-1},

\displaystyle r^{(1)}_{i,j}=0\qquad\text{otherwise}.

(5.9)

The truncation can also be seen from this expression.
∎

5.1
Identification with the KZ equation

The result given above is consistent with the four-dimensional limit of the operator
{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}
(see
[
10
, Theorem 5.6]
):

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}=1+hH_{4d}+O\bigl(h^{2}\bigr),

(5.10)

\displaystyle H_{4d}=\vartheta_{x}(\vartheta_{x}+1)+\frac{\Lambda-x}{1-\Lambda}(\vartheta_{x}+m_{1})(\vartheta_{x}+m_{2})+\frac{\Lambda}{x}\frac{x-1}{1-\Lambda}(\vartheta_{x}-m_{3})(\vartheta_{x}-m_{4}),

(5.11)

where
\vartheta_{x}=x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
.
Using (
5.11
) and defining
\varkappa
and
a
by

t=q^{\varkappa}
,
Q=q^{a}
,
20
20

20

We have scaled the Coulomb parameter
a
by
\epsilon_{1}
.

we can see that the four-dimensional limit of the Shakirov’s equation

\Psi(\Lambda,x)={\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}}\Psi\biggl(\frac{\Lambda}{t},\frac{x}{qtQ}\biggr),

(5.12)

takes the form that looks like the Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov equation

\displaystyle\varkappa\frac{\partial}{\partial\Lambda}\Psi(\Lambda,x)=\frac{H_{4d}-(\varkappa+1+a)\vartheta_{x}}{\Lambda}\Psi(\Lambda,x)=\biggl\{\frac{A_{0}}{\Lambda}+\frac{A_{1}}{\Lambda-1}\biggr\}\Psi(\Lambda,x),

(5.13)

where
A_{0}
,
A_{1}
are operators acting on variable
x
:

\displaystyle A_{0}=\vartheta_{x}(\vartheta_{x}-\varkappa-a)-x(\vartheta_{x}+m_{1})(\vartheta_{x}+m_{2}),

(5.14)

\displaystyle A_{1}=(x-1)(\vartheta_{x}+m_{1})(\vartheta_{x}+m_{2})+\biggl(\frac{1}{x}-1\biggr)(\vartheta_{x}-m_{3})(\vartheta_{x}-m_{4}).

(5.15)

Below, we will show the relation with the standard KZ equation more explicitly.

Following
[
24
]
, we write the
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}
KZ equation
for generic complex spins
j_{a}
and level
k
,
\varkappa=k+2=\log t/\log q
, in the form

\displaystyle\varkappa\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{a}}\psi=\sum_{b(\neq a)=1}^{n}\frac{\Omega_{a,b}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\psi,\qquad a=1,\dots,n,

(5.16)

\displaystyle\Omega_{a,b}=-(x_{a}-x_{b})^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{a}\partial x_{b}}+2(x_{a}-x_{b})\biggl(j_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{b}}-j_{b}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{a}}\biggr)+2j_{a}j_{b}.

(5.17)

The number of variables
z_{a}
,
x_{a}
can be reduced to
2(n-3)
, thanks to the

{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})\times{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
symmetry for
x_{a}
and
z_{a}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{n}x_{a}^{i}\biggl\{x_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{a}}-j_{a}(i+1)\biggr\}\psi=0,\qquad\sum_{a=1}^{n}z_{a}^{i}\biggl\{z_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{a}}+h_{a}(i+1)\biggr\}\psi=0,

(5.18)

where
i=0,\pm 1
and
h_{a}=j_{a}(j_{a}+1)/\varkappa
.

We will consider the case
n=4
, where the conditions (
5.18
) can be solved as

\displaystyle\psi=uvf_{1}(z,x),\qquad z=\frac{z_{12}z_{34}}{z_{13}z_{24}},\qquad x=\frac{x_{12}x_{34}}{x_{13}x_{24}},

(5.19)

\displaystyle u=z_{12}^{-2h_{1}}z_{23}^{h_{1}-h_{2}-h_{3}+h_{4}}z_{24}^{-h_{1}-h_{2}+h_{3}-h_{4}}z_{34}^{h_{1}+h_{2}-h_{3}-h_{4}},

\displaystyle v=x_{12}^{2j_{1}}x_{23}^{-j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}-j_{4}}x_{24}^{j_{1}+j_{2}-j_{3}+j_{4}}x_{34}^{-j_{1}-j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}},

where
z_{ab}=z_{a}-z_{b}
,
x_{ab}=x_{a}-x_{b}
.
Then the equations (
5.16
) for
a=1,\dots,4
are all equivalent and we obtain a single equation for
f_{1}(z,x)
.
Through the gauge transformation

\displaystyle f(z,x)=gf_{1}(z,x),\qquad g=x^{j_{1}+j_{2}}z^{-h_{1}-h_{2}}(z-1)^{\frac{2j_{1}j_{4}}{\varkappa}},

(5.20)

the KZ equation for
f(z,x)
can be written concisely as

\displaystyle\biggl\{\varkappa\vartheta_{z}-\vartheta_{x}(\vartheta_{x}-1)+\frac{x-z}{1-z}(\vartheta_{x}-j_{1}+j_{2})(\vartheta_{x}+j_{3}-j_{4})

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\frac{z(1-x)}{x(1-z)}(\vartheta_{x}+j_{1}+j_{2})(\vartheta_{x}+j_{3}+j_{4})\biggr\}f(z,x)=0,

(5.21)

where
\vartheta_{z}=z\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
.

In order to identify (
5.21
) with the four-dimensional limit of Shakirov’s equation,
we change the parameters as
21
21

21

Here
\tilde{a}=a+\varkappa
.

(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})=\biggl(\frac{-m_{1}-m_{3}}{2},\frac{\tilde{a}-1+m_{1}-m_{3}}{2},\frac{\tilde{a}-1+m_{2}-m_{4}}{2},\frac{-m_{2}-m_{4}}{2}\biggr),

(5.22)

and make a gauge transformation

\Psi=x^{-m_{3}}(z-1)^{\frac{(m_{1}+m_{3})(m_{2}+m_{4})}{2\varkappa}}z^{\frac{(m_{3}-m_{1})\tilde{a}-(m_{1}-1)m_{1}-(m_{3}-1)m_{3}}{2\varkappa}}f.

(5.23)

Then the equation (
5.21
) exactly agrees with (
5.13
)

\displaystyle\biggl\{\varkappa\vartheta_{z}-\vartheta_{x}(\vartheta_{x}-\tilde{a})+\frac{x-z}{1-z}(\vartheta_{x}+m_{1})(\vartheta_{x}+m_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\frac{z(1-x)}{x(1-z)}(\vartheta_{x}-m_{3})(\vartheta_{x}-m_{4})\biggr\}\Psi=0

(5.24)

by the identification
z=\Lambda
.

There are
2^{4}
such identifications. This is because the equation (
5.24
) (= the quantum
P_{\rm VI}
equation),
has
(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{4}\subset W\bigl(D_{4}^{(1)}\bigr)
symmetry

\displaystyle\{m_{1}\rightarrow m_{2},m_{2}\rightarrow m_{1}\}\times\{m_{3}\rightarrow m_{4},m_{4}\rightarrow m_{3}\}\times\{m_{1}\to 1-\tilde{a}-m_{2},m_{2}\to 1-\tilde{a}-m_{1}\}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\times\{m_{3}\to 1+a-m_{4},m_{4}\to 1+a-m_{3}\},

(5.25)

up to a gauge transformation of the form
g=x^{k_{1}}(x-1)^{k_{2}}z^{k_{3}}(z-1)^{k_{4}}(x-z)^{k_{5}}
.
Each
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
transform in (
5.25
) corresponds to the Weyl reflection with respect to the outer nodes
of
D_{4}^{(1)}
Dynkin diagram.

Remark 5.2
.

The relation between the KZ equation and the quantum
P_{\rm VI}
was studied by
[
42
]
.
The derivation of the KZ equation from the gauge theory is given by
[
47
]
.
The equation (
5.13
) was previously obtained from Virasoro five-point functions.

Appendix A
\boldsymbol{q}
-Borel transformation and refined Chern–Simons theory

In this appendix, we would like to explain a part of motivation for the original non-stationary difference equation of
[
51
]
.
Namely, we would like to elucidate the reason for introduction of
the operator
\mathcal{B}
in the form that it was introduced.

A part of motivation to study non-stationary difference equations comes from the most well-studied example,
which is the non-stationary Ruijsenaars–Schneider equation suggested by
[
34
]
.
This non-stationary equation is satisfied by the
K
-theoretic character of the affine Laumon space
of type
\widehat{\mathfrak{gl}}_{N}
. The character in question is a special function generalizing the Macdonald polynomial.

For Macdonald polynomials, a relation to Chern–Simons theory and knot invariants is well-known
[
2
]
.
The relation is obtained through so-called refined Chern–Simons theory.
This theory provides some of the most prominent equations satisfied by Macdonald polynomials,
among them a key role is played by the integral identity,

\displaystyle\oint_{|x_{1}|=1}{\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}\dots\oint_{|x_{N}|=1}{\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}x_{N}}{x_{N}}}\exp\Biggl(-\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{N}}{\displaystyle\frac{x_{i}^{2}}{2g}}\Biggr)P_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\prod\limits_{i\neq j=1}^{N}\prod\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}{\displaystyle\frac{(1-q^{m}x_{i}/x_{j})}{(1-tq^{m}x_{i}/x_{j})}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\mbox{const}\,T_{\lambda}~P_{\lambda}(t^{\rho_{1}},\dots,t^{\rho_{N}}).

(A.1)

Here
\rho_{i}=(N+1)/2-i
is the Weyl vector of
\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
, the partition
\lambda
is arbitrary, and
T_{\lambda}

is a certain product of
q
,
t
to the power of quadratic form of
\lambda
.

In
[
2
, Appendix B]
, it is explained that changing the variables via
u_{i}=\log(x_{i})
,
it is possible to trade the Gaussian exponents
{\rm e}^{-x_{i}^{2}/2g}
under the integration sign
for theta functions
\sum_{n}q^{n^{2}/2}u_{i}^{n}
.
This can be understood simply as the result of action on the rest of the integrand by a product of operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}u^{n}=q^{n^{2}/2}u^{n},

(A.2)

or equivalently (see (
1.4
)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}u^{n}=q^{n^{2}/2+n/2}u^{n}

(A.3)

in each variable
u_{i}
, and setting all
u_{1}=\dots=u_{N}=1
afterwards.
The linear difference between operators
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{B}
is inessential and amounts to a shift of variable.

In our view, equations for more complicated functions generalizing the Macdonald polynomials
(such as the
K
-theoretic character of the affine Laumon space) are most likely far-going generalizations
of the elementary equations for Macdonald polynomials. For this reason, when considering the non-stationary difference
equation for degenerate five-point conformal blocks of
q
-Virasoro algebra in
[
51
]
,
we paid attention to the following interesting fact. In
[
34
]
, the main part of the non-stationary
difference equation is the operator
q^{\Delta/2}
, where
\Delta
is a certain second order differential operator.
For the case of rank 1, i.e.,
\mathfrak{gl}_{2}
, the action of
q^{\Delta/2}
on a monomial of the form
(x_{1}/x_{2})^{n}

is proportional (up to factors of the form
q
,
t
to the linear power in
n
) to
q^{n^{2}}
.

From the perspective described above, it is natural to expect that this operator should be
understood as a product of two operators, each of which contributes
q^{n^{2}/2}
.
This is natural both from the perspective of Macdonald theory, where this operator (
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
or
\mathcal{B}
, equivalently)
arises in the integral identities, as well as from the perspective of Ruijsenaars–Schneider system,
where
\Delta
is a sum of
N
contributions, i.e., 2 contributions in the
\mathfrak{gl}_{2}
case.
This was the principal reason why in
[
51
]
we chose to look for the Hamiltonian operator
on the right-hand side of the non-stationary equation in the form (see (
1.3
))

{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\mathcal{B}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\mathcal{B}{\mathcal{A}}_{3},

where the two operators
\mathcal{B}
are not joined together but separate.
It eventually turned out to be the correct ansatz.

Appendix B
Lemma on the
\boldsymbol{q}
-Borel transformation

A proof of the following lemma is given in
[
10
, Appendix B]
.
Here we provide another more direct proof. In the following we use a shorthand notation
for the infinite product
\varphi(z):=(z;q)_{\infty}
.

Lemma B.1

(
[
51
]
)
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\cdot{1\over\varphi(\alpha x)\varphi(\beta\Lambda/x)}x^{n}={\varphi\bigl(-q^{1+n}\alpha x\bigr)\varphi(-q^{-n}\beta\Lambda/x)\over\varphi(\alpha\beta\Lambda)}q^{n(n+1)/2}x^{n},

(B.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{-1}\cdot{\varphi(\alpha x)\varphi(\beta\Lambda/x)}x^{n}={\varphi\bigl(q^{-1}\alpha\beta\Lambda\bigr)\over\varphi\bigl(-q^{-1-n}\alpha x\bigr)\varphi(-q^{n}\beta\Lambda/x)}q^{-n(n+1)/2}x^{n}.

(B.2)

Proof.

We will show the first relation (the second relation follows from the first one).
By a rescaling of
\Lambda
,
x
, it is enough to show

f(\Lambda,x):=\frac{1}{\varphi(-qx)\varphi\bigl(-\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}\mathcal{B}\frac{1}{\varphi(x)\varphi\bigl(\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}=\frac{1}{\varphi(\Lambda)}.

(B.3)

Recall that
\vartheta_{x}=x\frac{\partial}{\partial x},~\mathcal{B}=q^{\vartheta_{x}(\vartheta_{x}+1)/2}
and
p=q^{\vartheta_{x}}
.
First we note that, for any function
F(x)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\cdot x^{n}F(x)

\displaystyle{}=(px)^{n}F(px)=q^{n(n+1)/2}x^{n}p^{n}F(px)

\displaystyle{}=q^{n(n+1)/2}x^{n}F(pq^{n}x)=q^{n(n+1)/2}x^{n}\mathcal{B}\cdot F(q^{n}x).

(B.4)

Using this identity, we obtain the following difference equation:

\displaystyle f(q\Lambda,x)

\displaystyle{}=\frac{1}{\varphi(-qx)\varphi\bigl(-\frac{q\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}\mathcal{B}\frac{1-\frac{\Lambda}{x}}{\varphi(x)\varphi\bigl(\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}

\displaystyle{}=\frac{1}{\varphi(-qx)\varphi\bigl(-\frac{q\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}\mathcal{B}\frac{1}{\varphi(x)\varphi\bigl(\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}-\frac{1}{\varphi(-qx)\varphi\bigl(-\frac{q\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}\mathcal{B}\frac{\frac{\Lambda}{x}}{\varphi(x)\varphi\bigl(\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}

\displaystyle{}=\biggl(1+\frac{\Lambda}{x}\biggr)f(\Lambda,x)-\frac{1+x}{\varphi(-x)\varphi\bigl(-\frac{q\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}\frac{\Lambda}{x}\mathcal{B}\frac{1}{\varphi\bigl(q^{-1}x\bigr)\varphi\bigl(\frac{q\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}

\displaystyle{}=\biggl(1+\frac{\Lambda}{x}\biggr)f(\Lambda,x)-(1+x)\frac{\Lambda}{x}f\bigl(\Lambda,q^{-1}x\bigr).

(B.5)

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle f(q\Lambda,qx)

\displaystyle{}=\frac{1}{\varphi\bigl(-q^{2}x\bigr)\varphi\bigl(-\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}\mathcal{B}\frac{1-x}{\varphi(x)\varphi\bigl(\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}

\displaystyle{}=\frac{1}{\varphi\bigl(-q^{2}x\bigr)\varphi\bigl(-\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}\mathcal{B}\frac{1}{\varphi(x)\varphi\bigl(\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}-\frac{1}{\varphi\bigl(-q^{2}x\bigr)\varphi\bigl(-\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}\mathcal{B}\frac{x}{\varphi(x)\varphi\bigl(\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}

\displaystyle{}=(1+qx)f(\Lambda,x)-\frac{1+\frac{\Lambda}{qx}}{\varphi\bigl(-q^{2}x\bigr)\varphi\bigl(-\frac{\Lambda}{qx}\bigr)}qx\mathcal{B}\frac{1}{\varphi(qx)\varphi\bigl(\frac{\Lambda}{qx}\bigr)}

\displaystyle{}=(1+qx)f(\Lambda,x)-(qx+\Lambda)f(\Lambda,qx).

(B.6)

Comparing the
f(q\Lambda,qx)
given by (
B.5
)
|_{x\to qx}
and (
B.6
), we have

f(\Lambda,qx)=f(\Lambda,x).

(B.7)

Hence, from (
B.5
) we have

f(q\Lambda,x)=(1-\Lambda)f(\Lambda,x).

(B.8)

Since
f(0,x)=1
, we obtain

f(\Lambda,x)=\varphi(\Lambda)^{-1},

(B.9)

as desired.
∎

B.1
Relation to the pentagon identity

There is yet another proof of Lemma
B.1
,

\mathcal{B}\frac{1}{\varphi(\alpha x)\varphi\bigl(\beta\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}x^{n}=\frac{\varphi\bigl(-q^{n+1}\alpha x\bigr)\varphi\bigl(-q^{-n}\beta\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}{\varphi(\alpha\beta\Lambda)}q^{n(n+1)/2}x^{n},

(B.10)

and show its relation to the pentagon (quantum dilogarithm) identity.

(i) First we note that (
B.10
) follows from its special case
n=0
:

\mathcal{B}\frac{1}{\varphi(\alpha x)\varphi\bigl(\beta\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}=\frac{\varphi(-q\alpha x)\varphi\bigl(-\beta\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}{\varphi(\alpha\beta\Lambda)}.

(B.11)

In fact, we have

\mathcal{B}f(x)x^{n}=(px)^{n}\mathcal{B}f(x)=q^{n(n+1)/2}x^{n}p^{n}\mathcal{B}f(x)=q^{n(n+1)/2}x^{n}\mathcal{B}f(q^{n}x).

Apply this to
f(x)=\frac{1}{\varphi(\alpha x)\varphi(\beta\frac{\Lambda}{x})}
and
using (
B.11
) we obtain (
B.10
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\frac{1}{\varphi(\alpha x)\varphi\bigl(\beta\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}x^{n}

\displaystyle{}=q^{n(n+1)/2}x^{n}\mathcal{B}\frac{1}{\varphi(\alpha q^{n}x)\varphi\bigl(\beta\frac{q^{-n}\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}

\displaystyle{}=q^{n(n+1)/2}x^{n}\frac{\varphi\bigl(-q^{n+1}\alpha x\bigr)\varphi\bigl(-q^{-n}\beta\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}{\varphi(\alpha\beta\Lambda)}.

(ii) The equation (
B.11
) can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi(\alpha\beta\Lambda)}{\varphi(\alpha x)\varphi\bigl(\beta\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}

\displaystyle{}=\mathcal{B}^{-1}\varphi(-q\alpha x)\varphi\biggl(-\beta\frac{\Lambda}{x}\biggr)

\displaystyle{}=\mathcal{B}^{-1}\sum_{k,l=0}^{\infty}\frac{q^{k(k+1)/2}(\alpha x)^{k}}{(q)_{k}}\frac{q^{l(l-1)/2}\bigl(\beta\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)^{l}}{(q)_{l}}=\sum_{k,l=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\alpha x)^{k}}{(q)_{k}}\frac{\bigl(\beta\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)^{l}}{(q)_{l}}q^{kl},

(B.12)

hence it is enough to show

\frac{\varphi(\alpha\beta\Lambda)}{\varphi(\alpha x)\varphi\bigl(\beta\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)}=\sum_{k,l=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\alpha x)^{k}}{(q)_{k}}\frac{\bigl(\beta\frac{\Lambda}{x}\bigr)^{l}}{(q)_{l}}q^{kl}.

(B.13)

In terms of
q
-commutative variables
ba=qab

and
q
-exponential function
e_{q}(x)=\varphi(x)^{-1}
,
the equation (
B.13
) is equivalent to the quantum dilogarithm (pentagon) identity

e_{q}(b)e_{q}(a)=e_{q}(a)e_{q}(-ab)e_{q}(b).

(B.14)

The identity (
B.14
) can be easily derived as follows:

\displaystyle e_{q}(a)e_{q}(b)=e_{q}(a+b),

(B.15)

\displaystyle e_{q}(b)e_{q}(a)e_{q}(b)^{-1}=e_{q}\bigl(e_{q}(b)ae_{q}(b)^{-1}\bigr)=e_{q}\bigl(ae_{q}(qb)e_{q}(b)^{-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hphantom{e_q(b)e_q(a)e_q(b)^{-1}}{}=e_{q}(a(1-b))=e_{q}(a-ab)=e_{q}(a)e_{q}(-ab).

(B.16)

Appendix C
List of various
\boldsymbol{R}
matrices and their characterization

Since various
R
-matrices are used in the main text, we summarize them along with its characterization.

(1) The matrix
R^{\rm Sh}=(r_{i,j}(\Lambda))_{i,j=-n}^{m}
arising from Shakirov’s operator in (
2.6
):

\displaystyle q^{\frac{1}{2}i(i+1)}x^{i}\bigl(-d_{1}q^{i-m}x\bigr)_{m-i}\biggl(-d_{4}q^{-i-n}\frac{\Lambda}{x}\biggr)_{i+n}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{j=-n}^{m}q^{-\frac{1}{2}j(j+1)}x^{j}(-q^{-m}x)_{m-j}\biggl(-q^{-n}\frac{\Lambda}{x}\biggr)_{j+n}~r_{i,j}(\Lambda).

(C.1)

(2) The matrix
R^{\rm HG}=\bigl(R^{\rm HG}_{i,j}\bigr)_{i,j=0}^{\ell}
defined by
{}_{4}\phi_{3}
series in (
2.13
):

\displaystyle\biggl(q^{\ell-1}\frac{x}{\beta};q^{-1}\biggr)_{i}\biggl(\frac{\alpha x}{z};q\biggr)_{\ell-i}=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}\biggl(q^{\ell-1}\frac{x}{z};q^{-1}\biggr)_{\ell-j}(x;q)_{j}~R^{\rm HG}_{i,j}.

(C.2)

(3) The matrix
R^{\rm Ito}=\bigl(R^{\rm Ito}_{i,j}\bigr)_{i,j=0}^{n}
in Theorem
3.2
:

\bigl(q^{n-1}b_{1}x;q^{-1}\bigr)_{j}\biggl(\frac{x}{a_{2}};q\biggr)_{n-j}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\bigl(q^{n-1}b_{2}x;q^{-1}\bigr)_{n-i}\biggl(\frac{x}{a_{1}};q\biggr)_{i}R^{\rm Ito}_{i,j}.

(C.3)

Actually, these
R
matrices are not unrelated. When
\ell=n+m
,
the relation of
R^{\rm Sh}
and
R^{\rm HG}
is already proved in Section
2.1
, namely
we have (
2.14
). One may confirm it by making a change of the “dummy” variable
x
in (
C.2
)
to
-q^{-n}\Lambda/x
, which implies (
C
) up to a scaling constant.
On the other hand, with the identification

\ell=n;\qquad a_{1}=1,\quad a_{2}=\frac{z}{\alpha},\quad b_{1}=\frac{1}{\beta},\quad b_{2}=\frac{1}{z},

(C.4)

we have
R^{\rm HG}_{i,j}=R^{\rm Ito}_{j,i}
.

(4) The matrix
A=A^{\rm Ito}=\bigl(A^{\rm Ito}_{i,j}\bigr)_{i,j=0}^{n}
in Theorem
3.2
:

\displaystyle\biggl[{n\atop i}\biggr]_{q}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}\biggl(-\frac{q^{1-n}}{z}\biggr)^{i}\biggl(\frac{x}{z};q\biggr)_{n-i}\bigl(q^{n-i}x;q\bigr)_{i}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\biggl[{n\atop j}\biggr]_{q}q^{\frac{(n-j)(n-j-1)}{2}}b_{1}^{n}\biggl(-\frac{q^{1-n}}{a_{2}b_{1}z}\biggr)^{j}\bigl(q^{n-1}a_{2}b_{2}x;q\bigr)_{j}\biggl(\frac{q^{j-n+1}x}{a_{1}b_{1}z};q\biggr)_{n-j}A^{I}_{i,j}.

(C.5)

(5) The matrix
{\tilde{A}}=\bigl({\tilde{A}}_{i,j}\bigr)_{i,j=0}^{N}
for the normalized solution in Section
3.2
:

\displaystyle\bigl(q^{N-j}a_{2}b_{2}x;q\bigr)_{j}\biggl(\frac{q^{1-N}}{a_{1}b_{1}}\frac{x}{\Lambda};q\biggr)_{N-j}=\sum_{i=0}^{N}(x;q)_{i}\biggl(q^{i+1-N}\frac{x}{\Lambda};q\biggr)_{N-i}{\tilde{A}}_{i,j}.

(C.6)

(6) The matrix
{\bf R_{\mu,\nu}}=\bigl(\delta_{i+j,k+l}{\bf R_{\mu,\nu}}_{i,j}^{k,l}\bigr)_{i,j,k,l\geq 0}

for
U_{\mathsf{v}}\bigl(A_{1}^{(1)}\bigr)
:

\displaystyle\biggl(\frac{b_{\nu}x}{z_{\nu}};q\biggr)_{i}\biggl(\frac{q^{i}x}{z_{\mu}};q\biggr)_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\biggl(\frac{b_{\mu}x}{z_{\mu}};q\biggr)_{k}\biggl(\frac{q^{k}x}{z_{\nu}};q\biggr)_{n-k}{\bf R_{\mu,\nu}}_{i,j}^{k,n-k}.

(C.7)

This matrix
{\bf R_{\mu,\nu}}
is related to the matrix
R^{\rm HG}
by

{{\bf R}_{\mu,\nu}}_{i,n-i}^{j,n-j}=\bigl[R^{\rm HG}\bigr]_{\ell\to n,z\to b_{\mu}\frac{z_{\nu}}{z\mu},\alpha\to b_{\nu},\beta\to b_{\mu}}J,\qquad J=(\delta_{i+j,n})_{i,j=0}^{n}.

A significance of the matrix
{\bf R}
is that it satisfies the Yang–Baxter relation.
The following direct proof using (
C.7
) may be instructive.

Proposition C.1
.

The matrix
{\bf R}_{\mu,\nu}
satisfies the Yang–Baxter equation, namely for any
(i,j,k)
,
(i^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
such that
i+j+k=i^{\prime\prime}+j^{\prime\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}
,
we have

\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}{{\bf R}_{1,2}}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{i^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime}}{{\bf R}_{1,3}}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{i^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime}}{{\bf R}_{2,3}}_{j,k}^{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}=\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}{{\bf R}_{2,3}}_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime}}^{j^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime}}{{\bf R}_{1,3}}_{i^{\prime},k}^{i^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime}}{{\bf R}_{1,2}}_{i,j}^{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}.

(C.8)

Proof.

For
k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with fixed
n=k_{1}+k_{2}+\cdots+k_{s}
, define a rational function
in
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
as

B_{k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{s}}={\mathcal{S}}\Biggl(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{qt_{i}-t_{j}}{t_{i}-t_{j}}\cdot\prod_{a=1}^{s}\prod_{i_{a}=k_{1}+\cdots+k_{a-1}+1}^{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{a}}f_{a}(t_{i_{a}})\Biggr),

(C.9)

where

f_{a}(t)=\prod_{i=1}^{a-1}\frac{1-\frac{\beta_{i}}{z_{i}}t}{1-\frac{t}{z_{i}}}\frac{1}{1-\frac{t}{z_{a}}},

and
{\mathcal{S}}
is the symmetrization on variables
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
.
The denominator of
B_{k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{s}}
is

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{\mu=1}^{s}\biggl(1-\frac{t_{i}}{z_{\mu}}\biggr)

and the numerator is a symmetric polynomial in
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
whose degree in each
t_{i}
is
s-1
.
The functions
B_{k_{1},\dots,k_{s}}
form a basis of the linear space of such rational functions
of dimension
\bigl({n+s-1\atop s-1}\bigr)
.
Define the action of a permutation
\sigma
of
\{1,\dots,s\}
on
\{z_{a},\beta_{a}\}
as

s_{\sigma}=\bigl\{z_{a}\to z_{\sigma(a)},\,\beta_{a}\to\beta_{\sigma(a)}\bigr\},

then we have the connection relation

s_{\sigma}(B_{k_{1},\dots,k_{s}})=\sum_{\{l\}}B_{l_{1},\dots,l_{s}}{C_{\sigma}}_{\{k\}}^{\{l\}}.

Note that the matrix
C_{(a,a+1)}
is local in index
a
, i.e., it depends only on
k_{a}
,
k_{a+1}
,
l_{a}
,
l_{a+1}
and
z_{a}
,
z_{a+1}
,
\beta_{a}
,
\beta_{a+1}
,
since
s_{(a,a+1)}(f_{b}(t))=f_{b}(t)
for
a\neq b
,
b+1
. Then, due to the relation

s_{(2,3)}s_{(1,2)}s_{(1,2)}B_{\{k\}}=s_{(1,2)}s_{(2,3)}s_{(1,2)}B_{\{k\}}
,
the matrices
C_{\sigma}
satisfy the Yang–Baxter relation

C_{(2,3)}\bigl(s_{(2,3)}C_{(1,2)}\bigr)\bigl(s_{(2,3)}s_{(1,2)}C_{(2,3)}\bigr)=C_{(1,2)}\bigl(s_{(1,2)}C_{(2,3)}\bigr)\bigl(s_{(1,2)}s_{(2,3)}C_{(1,2)}\bigr).

(C.10)

Furthermore, by the locality, the matrix
C_{(a,a+1)}
reduces to
C_{(1,2)}
, and we have

C_{(a,a+1)}=C_{(1,2)}|_{z_{1}\to z_{a},z_{2}\to z_{a+1},\beta_{1}\to\beta_{a},\beta_{2}\to\beta_{a+1}}.

(C.11)

Thanks to the specialization

B_{k_{1},k_{2}}\big|_{t_{i}\to xq^{i-1}}=\frac{(q;q)_{n}}{(1-q)^{-n}}\frac{\bigl(\frac{b_{1}x}{z_{1}};q\bigr)_{k_{2}}}{\bigl(\frac{x}{z_{1}};q\bigr)_{k_{1}+k_{2}}\bigl(\frac{x}{z_{2}};q\bigr)_{k_{2}}},

the coefficient
{C_{(1,2)}}_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{l_{1},l_{2}}
(
k_{1}+k_{2}=l_{1}+l_{2}
) is obtained from

\frac{\bigl(\frac{b_{2}x}{z_{2}};q\bigr)_{k_{2}}}{\bigl(\frac{x}{z_{2}};q\bigr)_{k_{1}+k_{2}}\bigl(\frac{x}{z_{1}};q\bigr)_{k_{2}}}=\sum_{l_{1},l_{2}}\frac{\bigl(\frac{b_{1}x}{z_{1}};q\bigr)_{l_{2}}}{\bigl(\frac{x}{z_{1}};q\bigr)_{l_{1}+l_{2}}\bigl(\frac{x}{z_{2}};q\bigr)_{l_{2}}}(C_{1,2})_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{l_{1},l_{2}},

i.e.,

\biggl(\frac{b_{2}x}{z_{2}};q\biggr)_{k_{2}}\biggl(q^{k_{2}}\frac{x}{z_{1}};q\biggr)_{k_{1}}=\sum_{l_{1},l_{2}}\biggl(\frac{b_{1}x}{z_{1}};q\biggr)_{l_{2}}\biggl(q^{l_{2}}\frac{x}{z_{2}};q\biggr)_{l_{1}}(C_{1,2})_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{l_{1},l_{2}}.

(C.12)

Equations (
C.10
)–(
C.12
) give the desired Yang–Baxter relation and defining equation for
the matrix
C
(=
{\bf R}
).
∎

Appendix D
Proof of
\boldsymbol{RD_{2}A=ARD_{2}}

D.1
Recollection of Ito’s
\boldsymbol{R}
and
\boldsymbol{A}
matrices

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
22
22

22

The non-negative integer
n
is denoted by
N
in the main text.

and
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2},q,\Lambda\in\mathbb{C}
be
generic parameters.
Ito has introduced the matrices
R
and
A
(in
[
29
, Theorems 1.4 and 1.7]
) given as follows.
Remark that to have a consistent notation with the main body of our paper,
we change the notation as
t_{\rm Ito}\rightarrow q
,
(q^{\alpha})_{\rm Ito}\rightarrow\Lambda
.

Definition D.1
.

Let
R
be the
(n+1)
-dimensional square matrix given in the Gauss decomposed forms

\displaystyle R=L_{R}D_{R}U_{R}=U_{R}^{\prime}D_{R}^{\prime}L_{R}^{\prime},

\displaystyle L_{R}=\bigl(l^{R}_{ij}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq n},\qquad D_{R}=\bigl(\delta_{ij}d^{R}_{j}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq n},\qquad U_{R}=\bigl(u^{R}_{ij}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq n},

\displaystyle U_{R}^{\prime}=\bigl({u^{\prime}}^{R}_{ij}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq n},\qquad D_{R}^{\prime}=\bigl(\delta_{ij}{d^{\prime}}^{R}_{j}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq n},\qquad L_{R}^{\prime}=\bigl({l^{\prime}}^{R}_{ij}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq n},

where

\displaystyle l^{R}_{ij}=\left[n-j\atop n-i\right]_{q^{-1}}{(-1)^{i-j}q^{-\left(i-j\atop 2\right)}\bigl(a_{2}b_{2}q^{j};q\bigr)_{i-j}\over\bigl(a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}q^{-(n-2j-1)};q\bigr)_{i-j}},

\displaystyle d^{R}_{j}={\bigl(a_{1}a_{2}^{-1}q^{-j};q\bigr)_{n-j}(a_{2}b_{1};q)_{j}\over(a_{1}b_{2};q)_{n-j}\bigl(a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}q^{-(n-j)};q\bigr)_{j}},

\displaystyle u^{R}_{ij}=\left[j\atop i\right]_{q^{-1}}{\bigl(a_{1}b_{1}q^{n-j};q\bigr)_{j-i}\over\bigl(a_{1}a_{2}^{-1}q^{n-i-j};q\bigr)_{j-i}},

\displaystyle{u^{\prime}}^{R}_{ij}=\left[j\atop i\right]_{q}{(-1)^{j-i}q^{\left(j-i\atop 2\right)}\bigl(a_{1}^{-1}b_{1}^{-1}q^{-(n-i-1)};q\bigr)_{j-i}\over\bigl(b_{1}^{-1}b_{2}q^{i+j-n};q\bigr)_{j-i}},

\displaystyle{d^{\prime}}^{R}_{j}={\bigl(b_{1}b_{2}^{-1}q^{n-2j+1};q\bigr)_{j}\bigl(a_{2}^{-1}b_{1}^{-1}q^{-(n-j-1)};q\bigr)_{n-j}\over\bigl(a_{1}^{-1}b_{2}^{-1}q^{-(j-1)};q\bigr)_{j}\bigl(b_{1}^{-1}b_{2}q^{-(n-2j-1)};q\bigr)_{n-j}},

\displaystyle{l^{\prime}}^{R}_{ij}=\left[n-j\atop n-i\right]_{q}{\bigl(a_{2}^{-1}b_{2}^{-1}q^{-(i-1\bigr)};q)_{i-j}\over\bigl(b_{1}b_{2}^{-1}q^{n-2i+1};q\bigr)_{i-j}}.

(D.1)

Definition D.2
.

Let
A
be the
(n+1)
-dimensional square matrix given in the Gauss decomposed forms

\displaystyle A=L_{A}D_{A}U_{A}=U_{A}^{\prime}D_{A}^{\prime}L_{A}^{\prime},

\displaystyle L_{A}=\bigl(l^{A}_{ij}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq n},\qquad D_{A}=\bigl(\delta_{ij}d^{A}_{j}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq n},\qquad U_{A}=\bigl(u^{A}_{ij}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq n},

\displaystyle U_{A}^{\prime}=\bigl({u^{\prime}}^{A}_{ij}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq n},\qquad D_{A}^{\prime}=\bigl(\delta_{ij}{d^{\prime}}^{A}_{j}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq n},\qquad L_{A}^{\prime}=\bigl({l^{\prime}}^{A}_{ij}\bigr)_{0\leq i,j\leq n},

where

\displaystyle l^{A}_{ij}=(-1)^{i-j}q^{\left(n-i\atop 2\right)-\left(n-j\atop 2\right)}\left[n-j\atop n-i\right]_{q}{\bigl(a_{2}b_{2}q^{j};q\bigr)_{i-j}\over\bigl(\Lambda a_{2}b_{2}q^{2j};q\bigr)_{i-j}},

\displaystyle d^{A}_{j}=a_{1}^{n-j}a_{2}^{j}q^{\left(j\atop 2\right)+\left(n-j\atop 2\right)}{(\Lambda;q)_{j}\bigl(\Lambda a_{2}b_{2}q^{2j};q\bigr)_{n-j}\over\bigl(\Lambda a_{2}b_{2}q^{j-1};q\bigr)_{j}\bigl(\Lambda a_{1}a_{2}b_{1}b_{2}q^{n+j-1};q\bigr)_{n-j}},

\displaystyle u^{A}_{ij}=\bigl(-\Lambda a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}\bigr)^{j-i}q^{\left(j\atop 2\right)-\left(i\atop 2\right)}\left[j\atop i\right]_{q}{\bigl(a_{1}b_{1}q^{n-j};q\bigr)_{j-i}\over\bigl(\Lambda a_{2}b_{2}q^{2i};q\bigr)_{j-i}},

\displaystyle{u^{\prime}}^{A}_{ij}=(-\Lambda)^{j-i}q^{\left(n-i\atop 2\right)-\left(n-j\atop 2\right)}\left[j\atop i\right]_{q}{\bigl(a_{1}b_{1}q^{n-j};q\bigr)_{j-i}\over\bigl(\Lambda a_{1}b_{1}q^{2(n-j)};q\bigr)_{j-i}},

\displaystyle{d^{\prime}}^{A}_{j}=a_{1}^{n-j}a_{2}^{j}q^{\left(j\atop 2\right)+\left(n-j\atop 2\right)}{\bigl(\Lambda a_{1}b_{1}q^{2(n-j)};q\bigr)_{j}(\Lambda;q)_{n-j}\over\bigl(\Lambda a_{1}a_{2}b_{1}b_{2}q^{2n-j-1};q\bigr)_{j}\bigl(\Lambda a_{1}b_{1}q^{n-j-1};q\bigr)_{n-j}},

\displaystyle{l^{\prime}}^{A}_{ij}=(-a_{1}a_{2}^{-1})^{i-j}q^{\left(j\atop 2\right)-\left(i\atop 2\right)}\left[n-j\atop n-i\right]_{q}{\bigl(a_{2}b_{2}q^{j};q\bigr)_{i-j}\over\bigl(\Lambda a_{1}b_{1}q^{2(n-i)};q\bigr)_{i-j}}.

(D.2)

It was shown that a certain system of Jackson integrals satisfies two different types of
q
-KZ equations
described by two matrices
R
and
A

(see
[
29
, Proposition 1.2 (Matsuo) and Theorem 1.7]
),
which one may regard as a consequence of
the so-called base-fiber duality of the gauge theory.
Hence, the compatibility of the two dual systems
guarantees the commutativity of the pertinent matrices.

Theorem D.3
.

Let
R
and
A
be Ito’s matrices given as above.
Set

D_{2}=\bigl(\bigl(\Lambda q^{n-1}\bigr)^{i}\delta_{ij}\bigr)_{i,j=0}^{n}
.
Then we have
RD_{2}A=ARD_{2}
.

In this appendix, we give an alternative direct proof of this commutativity.

D.2
Notations

We use the standard notations for the basic hypergeometric
{}_{r+1}\phi_{r}
series

\displaystyle{}_{r+1}\phi_{r}\biggl[{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{r+1}\atop b_{1},\dots,b_{r}};q,z\biggr]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{(a_{1};q)_{n}(a_{2};q)_{n}\cdots(a_{r+1};q)_{n}\over(q;q)_{n}(b_{1};q)_{n}\cdots(b_{r};q)_{n}}z^{n}.

A
{}_{r+1}\phi_{r}
series is called balanced (or Saalschützian) if
b_{1}b_{2}\cdots b_{r}=qa_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{r+1}
and
z=q
.
We use the shorthand notation
{}_{r+1}W_{r}
for the very-well-poised
{}_{r+1}\phi_{r}
series

\displaystyle{}_{r+1}W_{r}(a_{1};a_{4},a_{5},\dots,a_{r+1};q,z)

\displaystyle{}={}_{r+1}\phi_{r}\left[{a_{1},qa_{1}^{1/2},-qa_{1}^{1/2},a_{4},\dots,a_{r+1}\atop a_{1}^{1/2},-a_{1}^{1/2},qa_{1}/a_{4},\dots,qa_{1}/a_{r+1}};q,z\right]

\displaystyle{}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{(a_{1};q)_{n}(a_{4};q)_{n}\cdots(a_{r+1};q)_{n}\over(q;q)_{n}(qa_{1}/a_{4};q)_{n}\cdots(qa_{1}/a_{r+1};q)_{n}}{1-a_{1}q^{2n}\over 1-a_{1}}z^{n}.

As for the detail, see
[
28
]
.

D.3
Andrews’ and Bressoud’s matrix inversion formulas

Let
{\mathcal{A}}(a)=({\mathcal{A}}_{ij}(a))_{i,j=0}^{\infty}
be Andrews’ infinite-dimensional lower triangular matrix
[
7
]
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}(a)={1\over(q;q)_{i-j}(aq;q)_{i+j}}.

(D.3)

Then the inverse matrix
{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}(a)=\bigl({\mathcal{A}}^{-1}_{ij}(a)\bigr)_{i,j=0}^{\infty}
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}_{ij}(a)={\bigl(1-aq^{2i}\bigr)(a;q)_{i+j}(-1)^{i-j}q^{({i-j\atop 2})}\over(1-a)(q;q)_{i-j}}.

(D.4)

Let
{\mathcal{D}}(a,b)=({\mathcal{D}}_{ij}(a,b))_{i,j=0}^{\infty}
be Bressoud’s infinite-dimensional lower triangular matrix
[
18
]

in the original form,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{ij}(a,b)={\bigl(1-aq^{2j}\bigr)(b;q)_{i+j}\bigl(ba^{-1};q\bigr)_{i-j}\bigl(ba^{-1}\bigr)^{j}\over(1-a)(aq;q)_{i+j}(q;q)_{i-j}}.

(D.5)

Then the inverse matrix is given by
{\mathcal{D}}^{-1}(a,b)=({\mathcal{D}}_{ij}(b,a))_{i,j=0}^{\infty}
.

Closely following the line in
[
3
]
,
one can state the interrelations between Andrews’ and Bressoud’s matrices
in the following manner, aiming at (
D.8
) and (
D.9
) in Corollary
D.6
below.
Consider the gauge transformation
{\mathcal{B}}(a,b)=({\mathcal{B}}_{ij}(a,b))_{i,j=0}^{\infty}
of Bressoud’s matrix

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{ij}(a,b)

\displaystyle={(aq;q)_{2i}\over(a;q)_{2i}}b^{i}\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{ij}(b,a)\cdot{(b;q)_{2j}\over(bq;q)_{2j}}a^{-j}={\bigl(1-aq^{2i}\bigr)(a;q)_{i+j}(a/b;q)_{i-j}\over(1-a)(bq;q)_{i+j}(q;q)_{i-j}}b^{i-j}.

(D.6)

Remark D.4
.

Our choice of the gauge transformation is different from the one in
[
3
]
.

Proposition D.5

(
[
3
]
)
.

We have the initial condition
{\mathcal{B}}(a,a)=(\delta_{ij})_{i,j=0}^{\infty}
and the transition property

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(a,b){\mathcal{B}}(b,c)={\mathcal{B}}(a,c),

(D.7)

which in the particular case
a=c
means Bressoud’s matrix inversion
{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(a,b)={\mathcal{B}}(b,a)
,
or
{\mathcal{D}}^{-1}(a,b)={\mathcal{D}}(b,a)
.

For the readers’ convenience, we reproduce the proof in
[
3
]
.

Proof.

Let
i\geq j
. We need to calculate

\sum_{k=j}^{i}{\mathcal{B}}_{ik}(a,b){\mathcal{B}}_{kj}(b,c)
.
In the following summation, by using the definition
(
D.6
), one finds the
summable very-well-poised
{}_{6}W_{5}
series
[
28
, formula (2.4.2)]
as

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{i-j}{{\mathcal{B}}_{i,j+l}(a,b){\mathcal{B}}_{j+l,j}(b,c)\over{\mathcal{B}}_{ij}(a,b){\mathcal{B}}_{jj}(b,c)}

\displaystyle{}={}_{6}W_{5}\bigl(bq^{2j};aq^{i+j},b/c,q^{-i+j};q,cq/a\bigr)

\displaystyle{}={\bigl(a/c,bq^{2j+1};q\bigr)_{i-j}\over\bigl(a/b,cq^{2j+1};q\bigr)_{i-j}}(c/b)^{i-j}.

Then simplification of the factors shows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{ij}(a,b){\mathcal{B}}_{jj}(b,c){\bigl(a/c,bq^{2j+1};q\bigr)_{i-j}\over\bigl(a/b,cq^{2j+1};q\bigr)_{i-j}}(c/b)^{i-j}=\mathcal{B}_{ij}(a,c).

Hence, we have (
D.7
).
∎

Corollary D.6
.

By taking the limits
b\rightarrow 0
or
\infty
in (
D.7
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k}a^{i}{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}_{ik}(a)a^{-k}c^{k}{\mathcal{A}}_{kj}(c)c^{-j}={\mathcal{B}}_{ij}(a,c),

(D.8)

\displaystyle\sum_{k}q^{-i^{2}}{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}_{ik}(a){\mathcal{A}}_{kj}(c)q^{j^{2}}={\mathcal{B}}_{ij}(a,c),

(D.9)

which reproduce Andrews’ matrix inversion by setting
a=c
.

Proof.

It follows from

\displaystyle\lim_{b\rightarrow 0}{\mathcal{B}}_{ik}(a,b)=a^{i-k}{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}_{ik}(a),

\displaystyle\lim_{b\rightarrow 0}{\mathcal{B}}_{kj}(b,c)=c^{k-j}{\mathcal{A}}_{kj}(c),

\displaystyle\lim_{b\rightarrow\infty}b^{2k}{\mathcal{B}}_{ik}(a,b)=q^{-i^{2}-k(k+1)}{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}_{ik}(a),\qquad

\displaystyle\lim_{b\rightarrow\infty}b^{-2k}{\mathcal{B}}_{kj}(b,c)=q^{k(k+1)+j^{2}}{\mathcal{A}}_{kj}(c).

∎

D.4
Expressions for
\boldsymbol{L_{X}}
,

Definition D.7

\displaystyle\alpha=1/a_{2}b_{2},\qquad\beta=1/a_{1}b_{1},\qquad z=1/a_{1}b_{2},\qquad w=\Lambda q^{n-1}.

(D.10)

Definition D.8
.

\displaystyle g^{U}_{i}=(z/\alpha\beta)^{i}\bigl(q^{-n+1}\beta;q\bigr)_{i}(q;q)_{i},

\displaystyle g^{\prime L}_{i}=q^{i}(z/\alpha\beta)^{-i}(1/\alpha;q)_{i}(q^{-n};q)_{i},\qquad

\displaystyle h^{\prime A}_{i}=\bigl(q^{-n+1}\beta/w;q\bigr)_{2i}.

(D.11)

Proposition D.9
.

We have

\displaystyle l^{R}_{ij}=g^{L}_{i}{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}(q^{-n}z/\alpha){h^{R}_{j}\over g^{L}_{j}},\qquad

\displaystyle l^{A}_{ij}=g^{L}_{i}{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}(q^{-n}w/\alpha){h^{A}_{j}\over g^{L}_{j}},

(D.12)

\displaystyle u^{R}_{ij}={h^{R}_{i}\over g^{U}_{i}}{\mathcal{A}}_{ji}(q^{-n}z/\alpha)g^{U}_{j},\qquad

\displaystyle u^{A}_{ij}={h^{A}_{i}\over w^{i}g^{U}_{i}}{\mathcal{A}}_{ji}(q^{-n}w/\alpha)w^{j}g^{U}_{j},

(D.13)

\displaystyle{u^{\prime}}^{R}_{ij}={1\over g^{\prime U}_{i}}{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}_{ji}(q^{-n}\beta/z){g^{\prime U}_{j}\over h^{\prime R}_{j}},\qquad

\displaystyle{u^{\prime}}^{A}_{ij}={1\over g^{\prime U}_{i}}{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}_{ji}(q^{-n}\beta/w){g^{\prime U}_{j}\over h^{\prime A}_{j}},

(D.14)

\displaystyle{l^{\prime}}^{R}_{ij}={g^{\prime L}_{i}\over h^{\prime R}_{i}}{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}_{ij}(q^{-n}\beta/z){1\over g^{\prime L}_{j}},\qquad

\displaystyle{l^{\prime}}^{A}_{ij}={g^{\prime L}_{i}\over w^{i}h^{\prime A}_{i}}{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}_{ij}(q^{-n}\beta/w){w^{j}\over g^{\prime L}_{j}}.

(D.15)

Proof.

Straightforward calculation by using the definitions in (
D.1
) and (
D.2
),
the parametrization (
D.10

the gauge factors (

Andrews’ matrices (

) and (

D.5
Matrices
\boldsymbol{R}
and
\boldsymbol{A}
and Watson’s transformation formula

for terminating very-well-poised
\boldsymbol{{}_{8}\phi_{7}}
series

Proposition D.10
.

We have

\displaystyle d^{R}_{j}={(\alpha/z;q)_{n}\over(1/z;q)_{n}}\cdot q^{j^{2}}(q^{n}\alpha)^{-j}{(z/\alpha\beta;q)_{j}(qz/\alpha;q)_{j}(q^{-n}z/\alpha;q)_{j}\bigl(q^{-n+1}z;q\bigr)_{j}\over(q^{-n}z/\alpha;q)_{2j}\bigl(q^{-n+1}z/\alpha;q\bigr)_{2j}},

(D.16)

\displaystyle d^{\prime R}_{j}=\alpha^{n}{(z/\alpha\beta;q)_{n}\over(z/\beta;q)_{n}}\cdot q^{-j^{2}}(q^{n}/\beta)^{j}{(q^{-n}\beta/z;q)_{2j}\bigl(q^{-n+1}\beta/z;q\bigr)_{2j}\over(1/z;q)_{j}(q\beta/z;q)_{j}(q^{-n}\beta/z;q)_{j}\bigl(q^{-n+1}\alpha\beta/z;q\bigr)_{j}},

(D.17)

\displaystyle d^{A}_{j}=(-a_{1}w/\alpha)^{n}{(1/w;q)_{n}\over(w/\alpha\beta;q)_{n}}\cdot{(\alpha/w;q)_{n}\over(1/w;q)_{n}}\cdot z^{j}q^{j^{2}}(q^{n}\alpha)^{-j}

\displaystyle\hphantom{d^A_j =}{}\times{(w/\alpha\beta;q)_{j}(qw/\alpha;q)_{j}(q^{-n}w/\alpha;q)_{j}\bigl(q^{-n+1}w;q\bigr)_{j}\over(q^{-n}w/\alpha;q)_{2j}\bigl(q^{-n+1}w/\alpha;q\bigr)_{2j}},

(D.18)

\displaystyle d^{\prime A}_{j}=(-a_{1}w/\alpha)^{n}{(1/w;q)_{n}\over(w/\alpha\beta;q)_{n}}\cdot\alpha^{n}{(w/\alpha\beta;q)_{n}\over(w/\beta;q)_{n}}\cdot z^{j}q^{-j^{2}}(q^{n}/\beta)^{j}

\displaystyle\hphantom{d'^A_{j} =}{}\times{(q^{-n}\beta/w;q)_{2j}\bigl(q^{-n+1}\beta/w;q\bigr)_{2j}\over(1/w;q)_{j}(q\beta/w;q)_{j}(q^{-n}\beta/w;q)_{j}\bigl(q^{-n+1}\alpha\beta/w;q\bigr)_{j}}.

(D.19)

Proof.

Straightforward calculation by using the definitions in (
D.1
) and (
D.2
),
and the parametrization (
D.10
).
∎

Definition D.11
.

Set

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}^{n}_{ij}(\alpha,\beta|z)

\displaystyle{}=\beta^{-i}{(q;q)_{n}(\alpha/z;q)_{n-j}(1/\beta;q)_{n-i}(\beta/z;q)_{i}\over(q;q)_{i}(q;q)_{n-i}(1/z;q)_{n}(1/\beta;q)_{n-j}}

\displaystyle\quad{}\times{}_{4}\phi_{3}\biggl[{q^{-i},q^{-n+j},z/\alpha\beta,q^{-n+1}z\atop q^{-n},q^{-i+1}z/\beta,q^{-n+j+1}z/\alpha};q,q\biggr].

(D.20)

\displaystyle R_{ij}=\mathsf{R}^{n}_{ij}(\alpha,\beta|z),

Theorem D.13
.

Proof of Proposition
D.12
.

Starting from the Gauss decomposition
R=L_{R}D_{R}U_{R}
,
one can proceed as follows. We have

\displaystyle R_{ij}=\sum_{k}l^{R}_{ik}d^{R}_{k}u^{R}_{kj}=l^{R}_{i0}d^{R}_{0}u^{R}_{0j}\sum_{k=0}^{\min(i,j)}{l^{R}_{ik}\over l^{R}_{i0}}{d^{R}_{k}\over d^{R}_{0}}{u^{R}_{kj}\over u^{R}_{0j}}=l^{R}_{i0}d^{R}_{0}u^{R}_{0j}\cdot X,

where the summation

), (

), and (

), (

), (

) below
as

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle{}=\sum_{k=0}^{\min(i,j)}{\mathcal{A}_{ik}(q^{-n}z/\alpha)\over\mathcal{A}_{i0}(q^{-n}z/\alpha)}{h^{R}_{k}\over g^{L}_{k}}{d^{R}_{k}\over d^{R}_{0}}{h^{R}_{k}\over g^{U}_{k}}{\mathcal{A}_{jk}(q^{-n}z/\alpha)\over\mathcal{A}_{j0}(q^{-n}z/\alpha)}

\displaystyle{}={}_{8}W_{7}\bigl(q^{-n}z/\alpha;q^{-i},q^{-j},z/\alpha\beta,qz/\alpha,q^{-n+1}z;q,q^{-n+i+j}\beta/z\bigr)

\displaystyle{}={\bigl(q^{-n+1}z/\alpha;q\bigr)_{i}(\beta/z;q)_{i}\over\bigl(q^{-n+1}\beta;q\bigr)_{i}(1/\alpha;q)_{i}}{}_{4}\phi_{3}\biggl[{q^{-i},q^{-n+j},z/\alpha\beta,q^{-n+1}z\atop q^{-n},q^{-i+1}z/\beta,q^{-n+j+1}z/\alpha};q,q\biggr].

Here, we have used
Watson’s transformation formula for
terminating very-well-poised
{}_{8}\phi_{7}
series
[
28
, formula (2.5.1)]
.

From (

), (

), (

), (

\displaystyle l^{R}_{i0}d^{R}_{0}u^{R}_{0j}

\displaystyle{}=g^{L}_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i0}(q^{-n}z/\alpha)d^{R}_{0}\mathcal{A}_{j0}(q^{-n}z/\alpha)g^{U}_{j}

\displaystyle{}=q^{i}(z/\alpha\beta)^{j}{(1/\alpha;q)_{i}(q^{-n};q)_{i}\over\bigl(q^{-n+1}z/\alpha;q\bigr)_{i}(q;q)_{i}}{\bigl(q^{-n+1}\beta;q\bigr)_{j}\over\bigl(q^{-n+1}z/\alpha;q\bigr)_{j}}{(\alpha/z;q)_{n}\over(1/z;q)_{n}}.

Hence, it holds that

\displaystyle R_{ij}

\displaystyle{}=q^{i}(z/\alpha\beta)^{j}{(\beta/z;q)_{i}(q^{-n};q)_{i}\over\bigl(q^{-n+1}\beta;q\bigr)_{i}(q;q)_{i}}{\bigl(q^{-n+1}\beta;q\bigr)_{j}\over\bigl(q^{-n+1}z/\alpha;q\bigr)_{j}}{(\alpha/z;q)_{n}\over(1/z;q)_{n}}

\displaystyle\quad\times{}_{4}\phi_{3}\biggl[{q^{-i},q^{-n+j},z/\alpha\beta,q^{-n+1}z\atop q^{-n},q^{-i+1}z/\beta,q^{-n+j+1}z/\alpha};q,q\biggr].

Proposition D.19
.

We have

\displaystyle{}={(1/\alpha,1/\beta,w/z,\alpha\beta/zw;q)_{n}\over(\alpha/z,\beta/z,1/w,w/\alpha\beta;q)_{n}}{\bigl(\beta/z,w/\alpha\beta,q^{-n+1}z/\alpha,q^{-n+1}w;q\bigr)_{i}\over\bigl(1/\alpha,w/z,q^{-n+1}\beta,q^{-n+1}zw/\alpha\beta;q\bigr)_{i}}

\displaystyle\quad{}\times{\bigl(\beta/z,1/w,q^{-n+1}z/\alpha,q^{-n+1}\alpha\beta/w;q\bigr)_{j}\over\bigl(1/\alpha,\alpha\beta/zw,q^{-n+1}\beta,q^{-n+1}z/w;q\bigr)_{j}}.

Proof.
